Ramification theory from homotopical point of view, I
Tomoyuki Abe

Abstract




Introduction

In the theory of
\mathscr{D}
-modules for complex varieties, the characteristic cycle plays an important role,
both in establishing the theory of
\mathscr{D}
-modules itself and applications.
In the context of the
\ell
-adic cohomology theory, similarities with the ramification theory were observed by experts including P. Deligne and G. Laumon.
Since then, establishing the theory of characteristic cycles in the étale setting had been an important challenge in the ramification theory of étale sheaves.
A breakthrough was brought by A. Beilinson and T. Saito in around 2015.
Beilinson defined the singular support for étale sheaves, and based on this work, Saito finally succeeded in defining the characteristic cycle.
Saito also established several functorial properties of the characteristic cycle, but the compatibility with proper pushforward,
which could be seen as a vast generalization of Grothendieck-Ogg-Shafarevich formula, remained as a conjecture
[
S2
, Conjecture 1]
.
One of our main goals of this paper is to solves the conjecture of Saito up to
p
-torsion.
Here is one of the main results of this paper (cf. Remark
6.6
):

Theorem
. —

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
,
and let
\Lambda
be a finite local ring whose residue field is of characteristic different from that of
k
.

Now, let
h\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism between separated
(1)
(1)

(1)

The theory of characteristic cycles works without separatedness assumption, and it is not too hard to lift this assumption by using Zariski descent.
However, to avoid unnecessary complication, we leave the details to the interested reader.

equidimensional smooth
k
-schemes of finite type.
Then we have the diagram
T^{*}X\xleftarrow{g}T^{*}Y\times_{Y}X\xrightarrow{h^{\prime}}T^{*}Y
.
For a closed subset
C
of
T^{*}X
, we put
h_{\circ}(C):=h^{\prime}g^{-1}(C)
.
Let
\mathcal{F}
be a constructible finite Tor-dimensional complex of
\Lambda
-sheaves such that the morphism

\mathrm{Supp}(\mathcal{F})\rightarrow Y
is proper.
Then we have the identity

\mathrm{CC}(\mathrm{R}h_{*}\mathcal{F})=h^{\prime}_{*}g^{!}\bigl(\mathrm{CC}(\mathcal{F})\bigr)

in
\mathrm{CH}_{\dim(Y)}(h_{\circ}\mathrm{SS}(\mathcal{F}))[1/p]
.

The case where
Y=\mathrm{Spec}(k)
and
h
is projective, the theorem has been proven in the fundamental paper
[
S1
]
.
In the case where
h
is projective, and
\dim(h_{\circ}\mathrm{SS}(\mathcal{F}))\leq\dim(Y)
(and slightly more conditions), the theorem has been proven in
[
S3
]
.
Unfortunately, contrary to the characteristic
0
situation, this inequality is frequently not satisfied (
e.g.
when
h
is the Frobenius endomorphism),
which demonstrates the complexity of the ramification theory for positive characteristic situation.
The biggest difference from this theorem of Saito is that, under the dimension assumption, we have

\mathrm{CH}_{\dim(Y)}(h_{\circ}\mathrm{SS}(\mathcal{F}))=\mathrm{Z}_{\dim(Y)}(h_{\circ}\mathrm{SS}(\mathcal{F}))
,
and the equality is an equality of actual cycles, whereas in the general case,
we must
construct
a homotopy between two cycles.
Very roughly speaking, we locally construct such a homotopy using the deformation argument of Laumon,
and we glue them together using the
\infty
-categorical machinery.
For simplicity, in this introduction, we assume
k
to be algebraically closed and
\Lambda
to be a field.

0.1

Let us briefly recall the theory of Beilinson and Saito.
Let
X
be a smooth scheme of dimension
d
over
k
and
\mathcal{F}
be a bounded constructible
\Lambda
-complex.
Then the characteristic cycle
\mathrm{CC}(\mathcal{F})
is the unique cycle
\sum n_{i}[C_{i}]
, where
C_{i}
is an integral subscheme of
T^{*}X
of dimension
d
such that
for any “isolated characteristic function”
f\colon U\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}
for an open subscheme
U\subset X
, we have

(0.1.i)

-\mathrm{totdim}_{u}\Phi_{f}(\mathcal{F}|_{U})=\bigl(\sum n_{i}[C_{i}],\mathrm{d}f\bigr)_{T^{*}U,u}

for any point
u\in U
.
This is called the
Milnor formula
.
Here
\mathrm{totdim}
is the total dimension, and roughly speaking, an isolated characteristic function is a function
f
such that
the formal sum
\sum_{u\in U}(\mathrm{totdim}_{u}\Phi_{f}(\mathcal{F}))\cdot[u]
defines a
0
-cycle on
U
.
Beilinson’s theorem on the existence of singular support implies that we have “enough supply” of isolated characteristic functions
so that we can determine characteristic cycle uniquely.
A difficulty of proving pushforward formula is that if we are given a proper morphism
h\colon X\rightarrow Y
,
even if we are given an isolated characteristic function
f
on
Y
with respect to
\mathrm{R}h_{*}\mathcal{F}
,
the pullback
h^{*}f
may fail to be isolated charateristic with respect to
\mathcal{F}
.
We revisit the construction of characteristic cycle, and define it without using Beilinson’s theorem and in a wider context.

0.2

Let us explain our method.
The pullback
0^{*}\mathrm{CC}(\mathcal{F})
in
\mathrm{CH}_{0}(X)
, where
0\colon X\rightarrow T^{*}X
is the zero-section, is called the

non-microlocalized
characteristic cycle
(2)
(2)

(2)

It is more common to call it
characteristic class
.

.
Following Beilinson
[
B1
]
, we reverse the order of the construction, and we construct the non-microlocalized one first.
We use homotopical argument for the microlocalization, but since the microlocalization process is essentially parallel to
[
B1
]
,
we do not go into the details in this introduction.

To explain the construction of non-microlocalized characteristic cycle,
let us introduce a (pre)sheaf
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}
on
\mathrm{Sch}_{/k}
of
flat étale systems
.
The definition of this sheaf is modeled on the definition of Suslin-Voevodsky’s relative cycle group in
[
SV
]
,
and we make use of the theory of nearby cycles over general base.
Let
X
be a
k
-scheme separated of finite type.
In Definition
4.8
(see also Remark
4.8
), we will introduce a sheaf
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}
having the following properties:

1.

The global section is
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X)
,
the Grothendieck group of the category of constructible
\Lambda
-modules on
X
.

2.

For a
k
-scheme
T
,
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(T)
is isomorphic to the subgroup of
\prod_{u\in\mathrm{geom}(T)}\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{u})
,
where
\mathrm{geom}(T)
is the set of generic points of
T
, consisting of
\{F_{u}\}_{u\in\mathrm{geom}(T)}

such that
F_{u}
are “good” and for any specialization map
u\rightsquigarrow t
, we have
\Psi_{t\leftarrow u}(F_{u})=F_{t}
.

On the other hand, consider the six functor formalism of motivic cohomology developed by Voevodsky, Ayoub, Cisinski-Déglise.
It associates to each
T\in\mathrm{Sch}_{/k}
an
R
-linear symmetric monoidal triangulated category
D_{T}
and they admit six fundamental operations.
Here
R
is a commutative ring such that
\mathrm{char}(k)^{-1}\in R
.
This triangulated category has an
\infty
-enhancement, which we denote by
\mathcal{D}_{T}
.
Using this formalism, we have the presheaf
\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
and the
\infty
-presheaf
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
sending
T\in\mathrm{Sch}_{/k}
to

\pi_{0}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(X_{T}/T)\simeq\mathrm{Hom}_{D_{T}}\bigl(h_{T!}h_{T}^{*}\mathbf{1}_{T},\mathbf{1}_{T}\bigr),\qquad\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(X_{T}/T)\simeq\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{D}_{T}}\bigl(h_{T!}h_{T}^{*}\mathbf{1}_{T},\mathbf{1}_{T}\bigr),

where
\mathbf{1}_{T}
is the unit object of
\mathcal{D}_{T}
.
In other words, these are relative Borel-Moore homologies.
Note that
\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}(k)\cong\mathrm{CH}_{0}(X;R)
.
Using these presheaves, our goal is to construct a (sensible) homomorphism
\mathrm{CC}\colon\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(k)\rightarrow\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}(k)
between the global sections.
We construct not only the morphism between the global sections, but a morphism of sheaves
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}\rightarrow\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
.
Let
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}\subset\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}
be the subgroup consisting of objects whose support is of dimension
0
.
Then by taking the rank, we may construct a morphism
\tau\colon\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}\rightarrow\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
using the trace map.
Since the characteristic cycle of a constructible sheaf with
0
-dimensional support should merely be the zero cycle associated to the rank,
we should have
\mathrm{CC}|_{\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}}=\tau
.
Now, we may ask ourselves how many extensions
\mathrm{CC}
of
\tau
there can be.
Surprisingly, if we replace
\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
by the (
\infty
-)presheaf
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
, we have the following extension theorem:

Theorem
. —

The restriction map

\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{h}\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/k})}(\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X},\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X})\rightarrow\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathrm{h}\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/k})}(\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0},\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X})

is an isomorphism, where
\mathrm{h}\mathcal{P}
denotes the category of
(
\mathbb{Z}
-valued
)

\infty
-presheaves.

Let us explain why.
For the time being, we use
\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
instad of
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
.
Assume that for each
F\in\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(T)
, we can find
F^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(T\times\Box)
, where
\Box:=\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{1\}
,
such that
F^{\prime}|_{T\times\{0\}}=F
and
F^{\prime}|_{T\times\{\infty\}}\in\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}(T)
.
Consider
\mathrm{CC}(F^{\prime})\in\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}(T\times\Box)
.
Since
\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant, we have the diagram

\textstyle{\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}(T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{*}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}(T)}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}(T\times\Box)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{\infty}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}(T),}

where
i_{x}\colon T\times\{x\}\hookrightarrow T\times\Box
is the closed immersion, and
q\colon T\times\Box\rightarrow T
is the projection.
The equivalence on the right implies that
\mathrm{CC}(F^{\prime})
is equal to
(i_{\infty}^{*})^{-1}\tau(i_{\infty}^{*}F^{\prime})
.
Thus, the diagram implies that
\mathrm{CC}(F)=\tau(i_{\infty}^{*}F^{\prime})
,
and the value of
\mathrm{CC}(F)
is determined once we fix
F^{\prime}
.
The element
F^{\prime}
can be regarded as a “deformation” of
F
, and this deformation “localizes”
F
to an element of
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}
at
\infty
.
We can summarize the above observation by saying that if we have enough supply of deformations, then the extension will be determined uniquely.

Now, how can we construct meaningful deformations?
In the celebrated work of Laumon
[
La
]
, he considered interesting deformations of
\ell
-adic sheaves inspired by works of E. Witten.
Following this idea, we will take
F^{\prime}
as
\mathcal{E}(F;f):=F\otimes\mathcal{L}(ft)
where
f
is a (local) function on
X
parametrized by
T
,

t
is the coordinate of
\Box
, and
\mathcal{L}
is the Artin-Schreier sheaf associated with a non-trivial additive character.
We remark that if we take
f
to be an isolated characteristic function with respect to
F\in\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(k)
,
then
\tau i_{\infty}^{*}\mathcal{E}(F;f)
coincides with the
0
-cycle in (
0.1.i
).
This comparison is a (rather straightforward) consequence of Laumon’s computation in
[
La
]
.

0.3

The actual situation is more complicated in various points, but in this introduction, we focus on the following two obstacles:

•

Unfortunately, the deformation
\mathcal{E}(F;f)
is not an element of
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(T\times\Box)
.
What we can say is that there exists a modification
T^{\prime}\rightarrow T\times\Box
over which
F\otimes\mathcal{L}(ft)
becomes an element of
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}
.
This deficit reflects the philosophy of the theory of nearby cycles over general base
that the situation is “good” only after taking a suitable modification on the base.

•

In order to kill the arbitrariness of the function of deformation, we need to consider iterative deformation,
and must show that this process terminates in finite steps.

To treat the first point, we introduce an
\infty
-presheaf
\partial\mathcal{F}
for any (
\infty
-)presheaf
\mathcal{F}
on
\mathrm{Sch}_{/k}
.
Intuitively,
\partial\mathcal{F}
is the sheafification of
\mathcal{F}
with respect to “modification topology”.
However, since the naïve modification topology does not behave well under base changes,
the definition is not just a sheafification of some topology.
Roughly speaking, a section of
\partial\mathcal{F}(T)
(more or less) corresponds to a section of
\mathcal{F}(V)
for some modification
V\rightarrow T\times\Box
.
Then, even though our
F^{\prime}
does not belong to
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(T\times\Box)
, it
does
belong to
\partial\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(T)
.
As a variant, we define
\partial_{x}\mathcal{F}(T)
(
x\in\Box
) to be the sheaf such that a section over
T

corresponds to a section of
\mathcal{F}(V\times_{\Box}\{x\})
for some modification
V
.
See
1.3
.
Using this operation, we may formulate the deformation cleanly, but the above strategy breaks down
since the morphism we get becomes
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}\rightarrow\partial_{\infty}\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
and
\partial_{\infty}\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
is not
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant anymore.
As long as we are considering the abelian presheaf
\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
, the author is not able to do more.
However, if we replace
\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
by the
\infty
-presheaf
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
,
we may find a section to the canonical map
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}\rightarrow\partial_{\infty}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
, called the
rewind morphism
.
A crucial difference between
\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
and
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
in the construction of rewind morphism is that
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
admits cdh-descents.
The construction of rewind morphism will be explained shortly.

For the second point, let us first explain why we need multiple deformations.
As we have seen, deformations are not unique, but we consider deformations parameterized by a space of functions.
Assume we are given
F\in\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(T)
, and two functions
f_{1}
,
f_{2}
on
X
parameterized by
T
.
Then we may consider deformations
\mathcal{E}(F;f_{1})
,
\mathcal{E}(F;f_{2})
.
Assume we are trying to extend
\tau
to
\mathrm{CC}
, and assume we are in a good situation where
\mathcal{E}(F;f_{1})
,
\mathcal{E}(F;f_{2})
belong to

\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(T\times\Box)
(not just
\partial\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(T)
) and even nicer
f_{i}
localizes
F
to an element of
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}
,
namely
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}(F;f_{i}):=i^{*}_{\infty}\mathcal{E}(F;f_{i})\in\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}(T\times\{\infty\})
.
Then the observation above implies that
\mathrm{CC}(F)
should be equal to
\tau(\mathcal{E}_{\infty}(F;f_{i}))
.
However, we have two choices,
i=1,2
.
This implies that to define the map
\mathrm{CC}
in this manner, we at least have

\tau(\mathcal{E}_{\infty}(F;f_{1}))=\tau(\mathcal{E}_{\infty}(F;f_{2}))
.
A benefit of considering presheaves on
\mathrm{Sch}_{/k}
is that the required information to construct the desired equality is already contained!
Indeed, we may take a function
g
on
X
parameterized by
T\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
such that the restriction to
T\times\{i\}
is equal to
f_{i}
.
Let
\mathrm{pr}\colon T\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\rightarrow T
be the projection, and we may consider the deformation
\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}F;g)
.
Assume for simplicity that we are in a lucky situation where
\mathcal{E}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}F;g)\in\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(T\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
.
If
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}F;g)
belongs to
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}(T\times\mathbb{A}^{1})
, then

\tau\mathcal{E}_{\infty}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}F;g)
yields the “homotopy” which gives the desired equality.
However,
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}F;g)
often does not belong to
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}
.
Nevertheless, we do not need to be desperate.
We may take a secondary function
g_{2}
, and try to deform
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}F;g)
using that function.
More precisely, we may consider
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}(\mathcal{E}_{\infty}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}F;g);g_{2})
and see if this belongs to
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}
or not.
If this one does not belong to
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}
, we take a tertiary function and we proceed this process.
In order for this strategy to work, one finds immediately that there should exist a sequence of functions of
finite length

g_{1},\dots,g_{n}

such that the iterated deformation
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}(\dots\mathcal{E}_{\infty}(\mathrm{pr}^{*}F;g_{1})\dots;g_{n})
belongs to
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}
.
One of the hardest fact to show in this work is that, in fact, we can always find such a finite sequence.
For the proof of this fact, we need new ideas, and the fact will be shown in Part II of this paper.
For more details, we refer to the introduction of [Part II].

Looking this construction closely, we notice that the following properties of

\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}
and
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
are needed for the uniqueness and existence of the extension:

1.

For any element of
F\in\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(T)
and for any function
f
on
X
parameterized by
T
, we have
\mathcal{E}(F;f)\in\partial\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}(T)

(functorially with respect to
F
).
Furthermore, we can always find a sequence
g_{1},\dots,g_{n}
so that
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}(F;g_{1},\dots,g_{n})
belongs to
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}
.

2.

The sheaf
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant and admits a rewind structure.

A pair of presheaf
\mathscr{S}_{0}\subset\mathscr{S}
which has properties
1
is called a
localization pair
,
and a (
\infty
-)presheaf having properties
2
is called a
reversible coefficient
(cf.
1.11
).
The above argument implies that given a localization pair
\mathscr{S}_{0}\subset\mathscr{S}
and a reversible coefficient
\mathcal{H}
,
any morphism
\mathscr{S}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
extends (essentially) uniquely to a morphism
\mathscr{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
up to canonical homotopy.

0.4

We have reduced the construction of the morphism
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
to
showing that
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}\subset\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}
is a localization pair and to constructing a rewind structure on
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
.
As we mentioned, the proof that
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}\subset\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}
is a localization pair will be given in Part II.
Let us explain how we endow
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
with rewind structure.
Note that, up till now, we have only used
1
-categorical argument, but this is one of the points that requires the use of
\infty
-categorical arguments,
since we need “gluing of cohomological elements”.

Recall that, for an
\infty
-sheaf
\mathcal{F}
, a section of
\partial_{\infty}\mathcal{F}(T)
corresponds (more or less) to a section of

\mathcal{F}(V_{\infty})
(
V_{\infty}:=V\times_{\Box}\{\infty\}
) for some modification
V\rightarrow T\times\Box
.
Thus, we wish to construct an element of
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}(T)
out of an element
F\in\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}(V_{\infty})
.
The first step is to reduce the construction to the case where
V\rightarrow T
is flat.
For this, we find a cdh-covering
\{T_{i}\}
of
T
and a modification
V_{i}\rightarrow T_{i}\times\Box
for each
i
over which
F
is defined and
V_{i}\rightarrow T_{i}
is flat.
This is, in fact, possible by construction of the functor
\partial
and using Gruson-Raynaud’s flattening theorem.
Then since the
\infty
-presheaf
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
is a cdh-sheaf, which is not the case for the abelian presheaf
\pi_{0}\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
,
it suffices to construct the elements for each
V_{i}\rightarrow T_{i}\times\Box
which are compatible in a suitable sense.
From now on, we assume that
g\colon V\rightarrow T
is flat.

For
\alpha\colon Z\rightarrow W
, let
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{c}}^{*}(Z/W):=\alpha_{!}\alpha^{*}\mathbf{1}_{W}
in
\mathcal{D}_{W}
.
Giving an element of
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}(V_{\infty})
is equivalent to giving a morphism
t_{V}\colon\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{c}}^{*}(X_{V_{\infty}}/V_{\infty})\rightarrow\mathbf{1}_{V_{\infty}}
in
\mathcal{D}(V_{\infty})
.
Using this cohomological interpretation, we wish to construct a morphism
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{c}}^{*}(X_{T}/T)\rightarrow\mathbf{1}_{T}
from
t_{V}
.
In the case
g
is flat, we have the trace map
\mathrm{tr}_{g}\colon g_{!}\mathbf{1}_{V}\!\left<1\right>\rightarrow\mathbf{1}_{T}
, where
\left<1\right>:=(1)[2]
.
Since
T\times\Box\rightarrow T
is smooth, the adjoint of the trace map yields
\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{c}}^{*}(V/T\times\Box)\rightarrow\mathbf{1}_{T\times\Box}
.
Taking the base change to
T\times\{\infty\}
, we get a map
\mathrm{tr}^{\prime}_{g}\colon\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{c}}^{*}(V_{\infty}/T)\rightarrow\mathbf{1}_{T}
.
Then we map consider the following diagram

\textstyle{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{c}}^{*}(X_{T}/T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{1}_{T}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{c}}^{*}(X_{V_{\infty}}/T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{\infty!}t_{V}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{H}_{\mathrm{c}}^{*}(V_{\infty}/T)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces,}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{tr}^{\prime}_{g}}

where
q\colon X_{V_{\infty}}\rightarrow X_{T}
and
g_{\infty}\colon V_{\infty}\rightarrow T
.
The dashed arrow is the morphism we are looking for.

0.5

Now, we are in the position to prove the non-microlocalized version of the pushforward formula.
Assume we are given a proper morphism
h\colon X\rightarrow Y
.
The commutation of the nearby cycle functor and
\mathrm{R}h_{*}
induces the morphism
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{Y}
.
By the functoriality of Borel-Moore homology, we also have the pushforward
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{Y}
.
For the pushforward formula, we need to construct the following (homotopy) commutative diagram of
\infty
-sheaves

\textstyle{\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{CC}_{X}}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{CC}_{Y}}
\textstyle{\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{Y}.}

Since
(\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0},\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X})
is a localization pair and
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{Y}
is a reversible coefficient,
if we fix a morphism
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{Y}
, extensions are all homotopic.
Thus, in order to construct the commutative diagram above, we only need to construct a commutative diagram for
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X,0}
.
This is just a property of the trace map, and we have the pushforward formula.

There are also other interesting consequences of our construction.
First, non-microlocalized version of characteristic cycle can be defined for any
k
-scheme
X
separated of finite type.
In the case where
X
can be embedded into a smooth scheme, this can be retrieved from Saito’s characteristic cycle, but in general, the construction seems to be new.
Another consequence is that the non-microlocalized characteristic cycle is compatible with nearby cycle functor.
Note that the singular support is
not
compatible with nearby cycle in general
[
S3
, 2.3.2]
.
This implies that the microlocalized version cannot be expected, unless we change the definition of the (microlocalized) characteristic cycle.
See Remark
5.14
for some explanations.

To conclude the introduction, let us outline the structure of this paper.
In §
1
, we formulate abstract formalism of the localization.
We define localization system as well as reversible coefficient and show that the map extends essentially uniquely.
In §
2
, we construct a rewind morphism on
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
, and show that
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X}
is in fact a reversible coefficient.
In §
3
, we introduce an abstract formalism for microlocalization, inspired by Beilinson.
In §
4
, we define the abelian presheaf
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}
.
For this, we need to use the theory of nearby cycles over general base, and we start by reviewing the theory.
In §
5
, we endow
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X}
with the structure of localization system.
Here, we assume the main result of Part II.
Summing up, we construct the characteristic cycle and show the pushforward formula for our characteristic cycle.
In the final section §
6
, we compare our characteristic cycle and the one defined by Saito.
In addition, we have 3 sections in the appendix, proving some technical results on
\infty
-categories.

We use the theory of
\infty
-categories, more precisely quasi-categories, for the most part of this paper.
Especially in §§
1
,
2
,
3
, we heavily use the
\infty
-category theory, but basic knowledge of
[
HTT
]
is sufficient for these sections,
and more technical results are shown mostly in appendix.

Notations and conventions

•

We fix a field
k
of positive characteristic.
We put
\Box:=\mathbb{P}^{1}_{k}\setminus\{1\}\,(\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}_{k})
.

•

A morphism
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
is said to be
birational
if it is proper and there exists an open dense subscheme
V\subset Y

such that
f^{-1}(V)\rightarrow V
is an isomorphism.
The morphism is said to be a
modification
if it is birational and each generic point of
X
is sent to a generic point of
Y
.

•

We denote by
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
the category of separated
S
-schemes of finite type.

•

In principle, we follow
[
HTT
]
for the terminologies and notations of
\infty
-categories.
Especially, by
\infty
-category we always mean quasi-category, and by category we always mean (ordinary)
1
-category.
Here are some exceptions.
We often identify (ordinary) categories with their nerves.
We denote by
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{pc}
the category of spaces.
We denote isomorphisms by
\cong
and equivalences in
\infty
-categories by
\simeq
.

•

Assume we are given topoi
\mathcal{T}
,
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
.
By
geometric morphism
from
\mathcal{T}
to
\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
, we mean a pair of adjoint functors
L\colon\mathcal{T}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}\colon R

such that
L
is left exact.
If we denote by
F\colon\mathcal{T}\rightarrow\mathcal{T}^{\prime}
the geometric morphism,
(L,R)
are denoted by
(F^{*},F_{*})
.
Let
\mathcal{C}
be a stable
\infty
-category equipped with t-structure.
As in
[
HA
]
, we use homological notation.
For example
X\in\mathcal{C}
, we denote
\tau_{\geq n}\tau_{\leq n}(X)\in\mathcal{C}^{\heartsuit}
,
where
\mathcal{C}^{\heartsuit}
denotes the heart of the t-structure, by
\pi_{n}X
.
This is the same as
\mathcal{H}^{-n}X
in the cohomological notation.

•

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an (
\infty
-)category admitting fiber products, and let
X\rightarrow Y
be a morphism.
If we are given another morphism
g\colon Y^{\prime}\rightarrow Y
,
we often denote by
X_{Y^{\prime}}
or by
g^{*}X
the base change
X\times_{Y}Y^{\prime}
,
if no confusion may arise.

•

For a stable
\infty
-category, recall from
[
HA
, 1.1.1.4]
that a cofiber sequence is a pushout diagram,
equivalently a pullback diagram, of the form

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z,}

where
0
is a zero object of
\mathcal{C}
.
We also denote this cofiber sequence simply by
X\rightarrow Y\rightarrow Z
if no confusion may arise.

1
Abstract formalism

1.1

Recall that a morphism
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
of schemes is said to be a
cdp-morphism
if it is proper and for any
y\in Y
,
there exists
x\in X
such that
f(x)=y
and the residue field does not extend.
A family of morphisms
\{X_{i}\rightarrow X\}_{i\in I}
is said to be a
cdp-covering
if there exists a finite subset

I^{\prime}\subset I
such that
\coprod_{i\in I^{\prime}}X_{i}\rightarrow X
is a cdp-morphism (cf.
[
GK
, 2.5 (8)]
).

1.2

Let
S
be a separated noetherian
k
-scheme (not necessary of finite type).
We define
\mathrm{Mdf}_{S}
to be the subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})
whose objects consist of morphisms

f\colon V\rightarrow T\times\Box
in
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
such that
f
is
proper
.
A morphism
(V\rightarrow T\times\Box)\rightarrow(V^{\prime}\rightarrow T^{\prime}\times\Box)
in
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})
belongs to
\mathrm{Mdf}_{S}

if the morphism
T\times\Box\rightarrow T^{\prime}\times\Box
is of the form
g\times\mathrm{id}_{\Box}
.
We define the topology on
\mathrm{Mdf}_{S}
to be the one generated by the coverings
\{(V_{i}\rightarrow T_{i}\times\Box)\}\rightarrow(V\rightarrow T\times\Box)

such that
T_{i}\xrightarrow{\sim}T
and
\{V_{i}\}\rightarrow V
is a cdp-covering.
The object
V\rightarrow T\times\Box
is often denoted by
(V\rightarrow T)
, or even simpler,
V
.
We may check easily that
\mathrm{Mdf}_{S}
admits fiber products. More explicitly, if we are given a diagram

(V_{0}\rightarrow T_{0})\rightarrow(V\rightarrow T)\leftarrow(V_{1}\rightarrow T_{1})
, the fiber product is

(V_{0}\times_{V}V_{1}\rightarrow T_{0}\times_{T}T_{1})
.
We let
b\colon\mathrm{Mdf}_{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
be the functor sending
(V\rightarrow T\times\Box)
to
T
.
The functor
b
is a Cartesian fibration of categories, and commutes with fiber products by the explicit description.

Remark
. —

The fiber of
b
over
T\in\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
is
\mathrm{Prop}_{T\times\Box}
, the full subcategory of
\mathrm{Sch}_{/T\times\Box}

consisting of objects
V\rightarrow T\times\Box
which is proper.
Endowing
\mathrm{Prop}_{T\times\Box}
with the cdp-topology, the fibration
b
becomes a Cartesian site (cf. Definition
A.7
).
The topology on
\mathrm{Mdf}_{S}
is nothing but the total topology.

1.3

Let
V\rightarrow\Box
be a morphism.
Let
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{O}_{V}
be the ideal generated by elements which are supported on non-flat irreducible components of
V
over
\Box
.
We put
V_{\mathrm{fl}}:=\mathrm{Spec}(\mathcal{O}_{V}/\mathcal{I})
.
Note that as a topological space
V_{\mathrm{fl}}=\overline{V}_{\eta}
where
\eta\in\Box
is the generic point.
The construction is functorial.
In particular, for a morphism
V\rightarrow T\times\Box
, we have the morphism
V_{\mathrm{fl}}\rightarrow T\times\Box
.
Thus, we have the functor
\mathrm{Mdf}_{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sch}_{/S\times\Box}
sending
V\rightarrow T\times\Box
to
V_{\mathrm{fl}}
.
Let
x\in\Box(k)
.
We have the diagram of
\infty
-topoi (where the morphisms are geometric morphisms)

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}(\mathrm{Mdf}_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{L}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Mdf}_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{fl}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S\times\Box})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{q}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{x}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}}

Here,
q
is the geometric morphism induced by the first projection
S\times\Box\rightarrow S
,

i_{x}
is the one induced by the closed immersion
S\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\times\{x\}}S\times\Box
,
and
L
is the topological localization (cf.
[
HTT
, 6.2.2.7]
).
We sometimes denote
\mathrm{fl}\circ L
by
\mathrm{fl}
abusing the notations.
We define functors
\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})
as

\displaystyle\partial_{S}:=b_{*}\circ L^{*}\circ\mathrm{fl}^{*}\circ q^{*},\qquad\partial_{S,x}:=b_{*}\circ L^{*}\circ\mathrm{fl}^{*}\circ i_{x*}.

We often abbreviate these by
\partial
,
\partial_{x}
respectively.
The morphism
q^{*}\rightarrow i_{x*}i_{x}^{*}q^{*}\simeq i_{x*}
induces the morphism of functors
r_{x}\colon\partial\rightarrow\partial_{x}
.
On the other hand, let
b_{\circ}(=b)\colon\mathrm{Mdf}_{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
be the functor sending
(V\rightarrow T\times\Box)
to
T
,

(q\circ\mathrm{fl})_{\circ}\colon\mathrm{Mdf}_{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
be the functor sending to
V_{\mathrm{fl}}
.
Then the functors
b^{*},(q\circ\mathrm{fl})^{*}\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Mdf}_{S})

can be identified with the “precomposition functor” with
b_{\circ}
,
(q\circ\mathrm{fl})_{\circ}
.
We have the morphism of functors
(q\circ\mathrm{fl})_{\circ}\rightarrow b_{\circ}
corresponding to the morphism
V_{\mathrm{fl}}\rightarrow T
.
This induces the morphism
b^{*}\rightarrow L^{*}(q\circ\mathrm{fl})^{*}
,
which induces the natural transform
I\colon\mathrm{id}\rightarrow\partial
by taking the adjoint.

1.4
Variant

This is a variant of the construction of the previous paragraph, and will be used in later sections.
Let
p\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
be a Cartesian fibration of
\infty
-categories.
Let us define the functors
\partial_{x},\partial\colon\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})
similarly to the previous paragraph.

We have the map
T\colon\mathrm{Mdf}\times\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
which is the adjoint to

\mathrm{Mdf}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{1},\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})
sending
(V\rightarrow T\times\Box)
to the morphism
V_{\mathrm{fl}}\rightarrow T
.
Note that
T|_{\mathrm{Mdf}\times\{0\}}=\alpha:=q\circ\mathrm{fl}
, and
T|_{\mathrm{Mdf}\times\{1\}}=b
, namely,
T
is a natural transform
\alpha\rightarrow b
.
Taking the
p
-right Kan extension of
b^{*}\mathcal{C}\times\{1\}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
along
b^{*}\mathcal{C}\times\{1\}\hookrightarrow b^{*}\mathcal{C}\times\Delta^{1}

where the latter is considered to be over
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
via
T
,
we have the functor
t\colon b^{*}\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\alpha^{*}\mathcal{C}

between Cartesian fibrations over
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
which preserves Cartesian edges.
Informally,
t
sends
c\in\mathcal{C}_{T}
over
(V\rightarrow T\times\Box)\in\mathrm{Mdf}
to
f^{*}c\in\mathcal{C}_{V}

where
f\colon V_{\mathrm{fl}}\rightarrow T\times\Box\rightarrow T
.
Since
b^{*}\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
is the base change of the Cartesian site
b\colon\mathrm{Mdf}_{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
, it is also a Cartesian site.
We endow
b^{*}\mathcal{C}
with the total topology.
We have the following homotopy commutative diagram of topoi:

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}(b^{*}\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{b}}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\alpha^{*}\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\alpha}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})}
\textstyle{\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}(\mathrm{Mdf}_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{b}
\scriptstyle{L}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Mdf}_{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}).}

Here,
\overline{b}
,
\overline{\alpha}
are the geometric morphisms induced by
b
,
\alpha
.
As before, we put
\partial:=\overline{b}_{*}\circ t^{*}\circ\overline{\alpha}^{*}
.
Similarly, for
x\in\Box(k)
, we have the diagram

\mathcal{P}(\alpha^{*}\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\overline{\mathrm{fl}}}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{Sch}_{/S\times\Box}})\xleftarrow{i_{x}}\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{C})

of geometric morphisms where
\overline{\mathrm{fl}}
is the morphism induced by
\mathrm{fl}
, and put

\partial_{x}:=\overline{b}_{*}\circ t^{*}\circ\overline{\mathrm{fl}}^{*}\circ i_{x*}
.

Let
T\in\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
, and assume we are given a functor
i\colon\mathrm{Sch}_{/T}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\times_{\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}}\mathrm{Sch}_{/T}

which preserves Cartesian edges over
\mathrm{Sch}_{/T}
.
Then the base change morphism
i^{*}\overline{b}_{*}\rightarrow b_{*}i^{*}
is an equivalence,
and we have an equivalence of functors
i^{*}\partial\simeq\partial_{T}i^{*}
.

1.5

Any (ordinary) category admitting coproducts is naturally tensored over the category of small sets.
For a category
\mathcal{C}
with coproducts,
X\in\mathcal{C}
, and a small set
I
, we denote this tensor by
X\otimes I
.
Concretely,
X\otimes I\simeq\coprod_{i\in I}X
.

Definition
. —

A
pre-localization system over
S
is a collection of data
(\mathscr{S},\mu,\mathcal{E})
satisfying the following conditions:

•

\mu
is a
pointed set-valued
presheaf on
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
, whose basepoint is denoted by
0
,
equipped with a morphism

c_{\mu}\colon\mu\rightarrow p_{*}p^{*}\mu
(
p\colon\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\rightarrow S
is the projection),
called the
contraction morphism
,
such that the composition
\mu\rightarrow p_{*}p^{*}\mu\rightarrow p_{*}i_{x*}i_{x}^{*}p^{*}\mu\simeq\mu

(
i_{x}\colon S\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
is the closed immersion for
x\in\{0,1\}
) is the identity for
x=1

and the constant morphism values at
0
for
x=0
;

•

\mathscr{S}
is an abelian presheaf on
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
equipped with positive exhaustive filtration;

•

\mathcal{E}
is a homomorphism
\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})\otimes\mu\rightarrow\partial\mathrm{gr_{\bullet}}(\mathscr{S})

of graded abelian presheaves on
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
.
Here,
\otimes
is taken as the underlying
set
rather than pointed set, namely,

\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})\otimes\mu\cong\bigoplus_{f\in\mu}\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})
.
As a convention, we put
\mathrm{gr}_{0}(\mathscr{S})=0
, in other words,
\mathscr{S}_{-1}:=\mathscr{S}_{0}
.

This data is subject to the following compatibility conditions:

•

The following diagram commutes:

(1.5.i)

\textstyle{\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}\times\{0\}}
\scriptstyle{I}
\textstyle{\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})\otimes\mu\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\textstyle{\partial\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}).}

A morphism of pre-localization system
F\colon(\mathscr{S},\mu,\mathcal{E})\rightarrow(\mathscr{S}^{\prime},\mu^{\prime},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
is a morphism of presheaves

F_{\mathrm{s}}\colon\mathscr{S}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}^{\prime}
which respects the filtrations and

F_{\mathrm{f}}\colon\mu\rightarrow\mu^{\prime}
(which we do
not
require to respect the contraction morphism)
such that the following diagram commute:

\textstyle{\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})\otimes\mu\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}}
\scriptstyle{F_{\mathrm{s}}\times F_{\mathrm{f}}}
\textstyle{\partial\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F_{\mathrm{s}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}^{\prime})\otimes\mu^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\partial\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}^{\prime}).}

The category of pre-localization system is denoted by
\mathrm{PreLSys}_{S}
or
\mathrm{PreLSys}
for simplicity.

Remark
. —

Note that, since the cohomological operations are used in the
\infty
-categorical setting,
the functor
\partial
is “derived” by default.
However, since
\partial
is left t-exact and
\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})
is an abelian presheaf,

\pi_{i}\partial\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})=0
for
i>0
, in other words, the higher homotopies vanish.
Thus, giving
\mathcal{E}
is equivalent to giving a homomorphism
\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}\mathscr{S}\rightarrow\pi_{0}\partial\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})
.
Namely, pre-localization system is purely a
1
-categorical notion.
This indicates that pre-localization systems are not too hard to handle, at least compared to
\infty
-categorical data.

Let us introduce some notations.
For
\mathbf{f}=(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\in\mu^{n}
and
\mathbf{g}=(g_{1},\dots,g_{n^{\prime}})\in\mu^{n^{\prime}}
,
we define the concatenation
\mathbf{f}\vee\mathbf{g}:=(f_{1},\dots,f_{n},g_{1},\dots,g_{n^{\prime}})\in\mu^{n+n^{\prime}}
.
Using the base point of
\mu
, we have a map
\mu^{m}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{id}\times\{0\}}\mu^{m}\times\mu\cong\mu^{m+1}

where the first
m
-components are identities.
The colimit over
m\in\mathbb{N}
is denoted by
\mu^{\infty}
.
Note that the operation
\vee
is
not
defined on the level of
\mu^{\infty}
.
For an abelian presheaf
\mathcal{F}
and sections
F\in\Gamma(T,\mathcal{F})
,
\mathbf{f}\in\Gamma(T,\mu^{\infty})
for
T\in\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
,
we denote by
(F;\mathbf{f})
the element of
\Gamma(T,\mathcal{F}\otimes\mu^{\infty})\cong\bigoplus_{\Gamma(T,\mu^{\infty})}\Gamma(T,\mathcal{F})
whose

\mathbf{f}
-component is
F
and the other components are
0
.
We put
\bm{\partial}:=\partial_{0}\oplus\partial_{\infty}
, and, recalling the notation
r_{x}
from
1.3
, let

\bm{\mathcal{E}}\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})\otimes\mu\xrightarrow{(-r_{0}\circ\mathcal{E},r_{\infty}\circ\mathcal{E})}\partial_{0}\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})\oplus\partial_{\infty}\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})=:\bm{\partial}\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}).

1.6

We have the homomorphism

\mathrm{gr}_{d}(\mathscr{S}\otimes\mu^{m+1})\cong(\mathrm{gr}_{d}\mathscr{S}\otimes\mu)\otimes\mu^{m}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{E}\times\mathrm{id}}\partial(\mathrm{gr}_{d}\mathscr{S}\otimes\mu^{m}).

Informally, this sends
F\vee f\vee\mathbf{g}
to
\mathcal{E}(F;f)\vee\mathbf{g}
.
Passing to the colimit over
m
, we have the map
\mathrm{gr}_{d}(\mathscr{S}\otimes\mu^{\infty})\rightarrow\partial(\mathrm{gr}_{d}\mathscr{S}\otimes\mu^{\infty})
,
which we also denoted by
\mathcal{E}
. Using this, let

\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}:=(r_{0}\circ I,0)+\bm{\mathcal{E}}=(r_{0}\circ I-r_{0}\circ\mathcal{E},r_{\infty}\circ\mathcal{E})\colon\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}\otimes\mu^{\infty})\rightarrow\bm{\partial}\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}\otimes\mu^{\infty}).

We put
\mathrm{Adm}_{d}:=\bigcup_{l}\mathrm{Ker}(\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}^{l})
.
Now, here is one of the main definitions of this paper:

Definition
. —

A
localization system
is a pre-localization system
(\mathscr{S}_{\bullet},\mathcal{E},\mu)

that satisfies the following condition:
For
F\in\mathscr{S}_{d}(T)
and
\mathbf{f}\in\mu^{l}(T\times\Delta^{m}_{\mathrm{geom}})
for some
l,m\geq 0
,
we can find a cdh-covering
\{T_{i}\}\rightarrow T
and
\mathbf{g}_{i}\in\mu^{l^{\prime}}(T_{i})
for some
l^{\prime}

such that
(F;\mathbf{f}\vee\mathbf{g}_{i})\in\mathrm{Adm}_{d}(T_{i}\times\Delta^{m}_{\mathrm{geom}})
.
We denote by
\mathrm{LSys}
the full subcategory of
\mathrm{PreLSys}
spanned by localization systems.

Remark
. —

1.

Since
\bm{\partial}
is left t-exact and
\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S})
is an abelian sheaf,

\pi_{n}\bigl(\bm{\partial}^{l}\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}\otimes\mu^{\infty})\bigr)=0
for
n>0
.
This implies that for a subsheaf
A\subset\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{S}\otimes\mu^{\infty})
, the condition

\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}^{l}|_{A}=0
is equivalent to
\pi_{0}\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}\vphantom{\mathcal{E}}^{l}|_{A}=0
.
Thus, the condition can be expressed entirely in terms of (
1
-)sheaves.

2.

When we are given a localization system, the following slightly stronger condition holds:
For a finite set
\{F_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq n}
of elements in
\mathscr{S}_{d}(T)

and
\{\mathbf{f}_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq n}
in
\mu^{l}(T\times\Delta^{m}_{\mathrm{geom}})
for some
l,m\geq 0
,
we can find a cdh-covering
\{T_{i}\}\rightarrow T
and
\mathbf{g}_{i}\in\mu^{l^{\prime}}(T_{i})
for some
l^{\prime}

(not depending on
1\leq k\leq n
)
such that
(F_{k};\mathbf{f}_{k}\vee\mathbf{g}_{i})\in\mathrm{Adm}_{d}(T_{i}\times\Delta^{m}_{\mathrm{geom}})
.
Indeed, we use the induction on
n
.
Assume the condition holds for
n<N
.
For
\{F_{k}\}_{1\leq k\leq N-1}
, we may choose a finite cdh-covering
\{T_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\mathbf{g}_{i}
.
By definition of localization system, for
F\in\mathscr{S}_{d}(T_{i})
and
\mathbf{f}_{N}\vee\mathbf{g}_{i}\in\mu^{l+l^{\prime}}(T_{i}\times\Delta^{m}_{\mathrm{geom}})
,
we can take a finite cdh-covering
\{T_{i,j}\}_{j\in J_{i}}
of
T_{i}
and
\mathbf{g}_{i,j}

such that
(F;\mathbf{f}_{N}\vee\mathbf{g}_{i}\vee\mathbf{g}_{i,j})\in\mathrm{Adm}_{d}(T_{i,j}\times\Delta^{m}_{\mathrm{geom}})
.
Thus the cdh-covering
\{T_{i,j}\}_{i\in I,j\in J_{i}}
and
\mathbf{g}_{i}\vee\mathbf{g}_{i,j}
meet our need.

Example
. —

Let
\mathscr{S}
be an abelian presheaf on
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
, and
\mu
be an abelian presheaf equipped with contraction morphism.
We put the
constant filtration
, namely
\mathscr{S}_{n}=\mathscr{S}
for any
n\geq 0
.
This filtered abelian sheaf is denoted by
\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{cons}}
.
Then
(\mathscr{S}_{\mathrm{cons}},0,\mu)
is a localization system, since
\mathrm{gr}_{d}(\mathscr{S})=0
for any
d\geq 0
.
We denote by
\mathrm{LSys}_{0}
the full subcategory of
\mathrm{LSys}
spanned by objects of this form.
The inclusion
\iota\colon\mathrm{LSys}_{0}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{LSys}
admits a right adjoint
z\colon\mathrm{LSys}\rightarrow\mathrm{LSys}_{0}

sending
(\mathscr{S},\mathcal{E},\mu)
to
(\mathscr{S}_{0,\mathrm{cons}},0,\mu)
.

From now on, we fix a presentable stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{D}
.
In practice,
\mathcal{D}

\mathrm{Mod}_{R}

1.9

For a stable
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, the
\infty
-category of
positively filtered objects
is defined to be

\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{fil}}:=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{N}(\mathbb{N}),\mathcal{C})
, following
[
HA
, 1.2.2]
.
This is stable as well by
[
HA
, 1.1.3.1]
.
Now, we consider the following functor

\mathrm{S}\colon\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{fil}}:=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{N}(\mathbb{N}),\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{RKE}}\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{N}(\mathbb{N})^{\triangleleft},\mathcal{C})\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{N}(\mathbb{N}),\mathcal{C})=:\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{fil}},

where the first functor is given by the right Kan extension along the inclusion
\iota\colon\mathrm{N}(\mathbb{N})\hookrightarrow\mathrm{N}(\mathbb{N})^{\triangleleft}
,
and the second is induced by the isomorphism
s\colon\mathrm{N}(\mathbb{N})^{\triangleleft}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{N}(\mathbb{N})

sending the cone point to
0\in\mathbb{N}
.
This functor is called the
shift functor
.
This is right adjoint to the functor sending
X_{\bullet}
to
X_{\bullet+1}
, and informally,
it sends a filtered object
X_{\bullet}
to
X^{\prime}_{\bullet}
where
X^{\prime}_{\bullet}=X_{\bullet-1}
(and
X^{\prime}_{0}=X_{-1}:=X_{0}
).
Now, we have the morphism of functors
\iota\rightarrow s^{-1}
.
This morphism induces the morphism of functors
\mathrm{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{id}
.
We define
\mathrm{gr}:=\mathrm{cofib}(\mathrm{S}\rightarrow\mathrm{id})
.

Remark
. —

Assume that
\mathcal{C}
is a Grothendieck abelian category, and let
\iota\colon\mathcal{C}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{C})

be the fully faithful inclusion to the derived
\infty
-category
[
HA
, 1.3.5.21]
.
If
X
is a positively filtered object in
\mathcal{C}
(in the usual sense), then

\iota(\mathrm{gr}_{\bullet}(X))
coincides with
\mathrm{gr}(\iota(X))
, since the sequence

\iota(X_{n})\rightarrow\iota(X_{n+1})\rightarrow\iota(\mathrm{gr}_{n+1}(X))
is a cofiber sequence.

For an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{X}
, we denote
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{X})^{\mathrm{fil}}
by
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{X})
.
We have

\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{X}):=\mathrm{Fun}\bigl(\mathrm{N}(\mathbb{N}),\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathcal{X})\bigr)\cong\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{fil}}}(\mathcal{X}).

Note that
\mathcal{D}^{\mathrm{fil}}
remains to be a stable presentable
\infty
-category by
[
HTT
, 5.5.3.6]
.

1.10

It will be convenient to upgrade the notion of pre-localization system to the case where
\mathscr{S}
is possibly an
\infty
-presheaf,
so that (a part of the data of) reversible coefficients are included.
In the following, we often abbreviate
\mathcal{P}^{\bullet}_{\star}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})
by
\mathcal{P}^{\bullet}_{\star}
.

Definition
. —

Let
\mathrm{Cont}
be the full subcategory of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{Ab}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})
, where
\mathrm{Ab}
denotes the category of abelian groups,
spanned by sheaves which admit a contraction morphism.
We have the functor
F\colon\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}}\times\mathrm{Cont}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Lambda^{2}_{0},\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}})
,
which sends
(\mathcal{F},\mu)
to the diagram

(\mathrm{gr}\mathcal{F}\otimes\mu\xleftarrow{\mathrm{id}\times\{0\}}\mathrm{gr}\mathcal{F}\xrightarrow{I}\partial\mathrm{gr}\mathcal{F})

(where the vertices correspond to the vertices
(1\leftarrow 0\rightarrow 2)
of
\Lambda^{2}_{0}
).
We define

\mathrm{PreLSys}^{\dagger}:=(\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}}\times\mathrm{Cont})\times_{F,\mathrm{Fun}(\Lambda^{2}_{0},\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}})}\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{2},\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}}).

We have the obvious fully faithful functor
\mathrm{PreLSys}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{PreLSys}^{\dagger}
.

Remark
. —

1.

Since the map
\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{2},\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Lambda^{2}_{0},\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}})

is an inner fibration by
[

, 2.3.2.5]
, the simplicial set
\mathrm{PreLSys}^{\dagger}
is an
\infty
-category.

2.

It would be possible to define a notion of
\infty
-categorical localization system allowing
\infty
-presheaves for
\mathscr{S}
.
However, this
\infty
-category may not be a full subcategory of
\mathrm{PreLSys}^{\dagger}
.
In the definition of localization system, we defined it in a way that the kernel of
\widetilde{\bm{\mathcal{E}}}\vphantom{E}^{l}
is “sufficiently large”.
For
\infty
-categorical localization system, being
0
is not a condition,
and we need to add the data of homotopies between
0
and the image of this “kernel”.

Let
\mathrm{PreLSys}^{\dagger}_{0}
be the full subcategory of
\mathrm{PreLSys}^{\dagger}
spanned by vertices

(\mathscr{S},\mu,\mathcal{E})
such that the filtration of

is constant
(

is equivalence for any

).
Evaluation at

induces the functor
\mathrm{ev}_{0}\colon\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}:=\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})
.
Thus, we have the the functors
\mathrm{PreLSys}^{\dagger}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}\times\mathrm{Cont}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{ev_{0}\times\mathrm{id}}}\mathcal{P}\times\mathrm{Cont}
.
The composition is a categorical equivalence.
Indeed, let
\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}_{\mathrm{c}}\subset\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}
be the full subcategory spanned by constantly filtered objects,
and
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}
be the full subcategory spanned by zero objects.
Then
\mathrm{PreLSys}^{\dagger}_{0}
is isomorphic to the simplicial set

(\mathcal{P}^{\mathrm{fil}}_{\mathrm{c}}\times\mathrm{Cont})\times_{\mathrm{Fun}(\Lambda^{2}_{0},\mathcal{Z})}\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{2},\mathcal{Z}).

Now,
\mathcal{Z}
is Kan contractible by
[
HTT
, 1.2.12.9]
and

is a trivial fibration by
[

, 4.3.2.15]
, thus the claim follows since the map

\mathrm{Fun}(\Lambda^{2}_{0},\mathcal{Z})\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Delta^{2},\mathcal{Z})
is a categorical fibration by
[
HTT
, 3.1.2.3]
.

We have the diagram of functors
\mathrm{Coef}\xrightarrow{h\times\{0\}}\mathcal{P}\times\mathrm{Cont}\xleftarrow{\sim}\mathrm{PreLSys}^{\dagger}_{0}

using the notation of Definition
1.7

,
and by choosing a quasi-inverse to the categorical equivalence, we have a functor

.

by choosing a quasi-inverse to the second equivalent morphism.

Our main result of this section is as follows:

1.11 Theorem
. —

The functor
\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathcal{X}_{0}\times_{\mathrm{LSys}_{0}}\mathrm{LSys}
is an equivalence of
\infty
-categories over

\mathrm{LSys}\times\mathrm{Coef}
.

Unwinding the definition, the fiber of
\mathcal{X}_{0}\times_{\mathrm{LSys}_{0}}\mathrm{LSys}
over

((\mathscr{S}_{\bullet},\mu,\mathcal{E}),\mathcal{H})\in\mathrm{LSys}\times\mathrm{Coef}
is equivalent to
\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathscr{S}_{0},\mathcal{H})
, and the restriction map

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathscr{S},\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{P}}(\mathscr{S}_{0},\mathcal{H})
induced by the map of bifibrations is simply the restriction map.
The theorem states that this map is an equivalence.
Note that, since the restriction morphism is independent of the structure of localization system nor the rewind structure,
the
existence
of these structures on
\mathscr{S}
and
\mathcal{H}
ensure that the restriction morphism is an equivalence.

As an immediate corollary of the theorem, let us transform it into a more practical form.
A pair of an abelian presheaf
\mathscr{S}\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{Ab}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})
and its subsheaf
\mathscr{S}_{0}\subset\mathscr{S}

is said to be a
localization pair
if there
exists
a localization system
(\mathscr{S}_{\bullet},\mu,\mathcal{E})

such that the 0-th piece of
\mathscr{S}
is
\mathscr{S}_{0}
.
Likewise, a
\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}
-valued cdh-sheaf
\mathcal{H}
is said to be
reversible
if there exists a reversible coefficient
whose underlying sheaf coincides with
\mathcal{H}
.
In
2.1
, we will introduce a coCartesian fibration
\mathcal{S}_{\pi^{*}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}^{\mathrm{op}}

whose fiber over
T\in\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}^{\mathrm{op}}
is equivalent to
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/T})
,
and coCartesian edges are given by the pullback
f^{*}
for
f\colon T^{\prime}\rightarrow T
in
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
.

Corollary
. —

Let
\mathcal{E}
be an
\infty
-category, and assume we are given 3 functors

\mathscr{S}_{0},\mathscr{S},\mathcal{H}\colon\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{\pi^{*}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}}
.
We assume we are given morphisms
\mathscr{S}\xleftarrow{i}\mathscr{S}_{0}\xrightarrow{C}\mathcal{H}
of functors.
Furthermore, we assume the following:

•

For each vertex
e\in\mathcal{E}
, the pair
(\mathscr{S}_{0}(e)\xrightarrow{i}\mathscr{S}(e))
is an localization pair;

•

For each vertex
e\in\mathcal{E}
,
\mathcal{H}(e)
is reversible.

Then there exists a morphism
\widetilde{C}\colon\mathscr{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
,
unique up to contractible space of choices, extending
C
.

Proof.

The morphism
i
induces the morphism

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{S}_{\pi^{*}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}})}(\mathscr{S},\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{S}_{\pi^{*}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}})}(\mathscr{S}_{0},\mathcal{H}).

It suffices to show that this morphism is an equivalence of spaces.
In view of Lemma
Lemma
, it suffices to show that for any morphism
x\rightarrow y
in
\mathcal{E}

the induced morphism

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{S}_{\pi^{*}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}}}(\mathscr{S}(x),\mathcal{H}(y))\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}_{\mathcal{S}_{\pi^{*}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}}}(\mathscr{S}_{0}(x),\mathcal{H}(y))

is an equivalence.
Let
T_{x}\leftarrow T_{y}\colon f
be the image of
x\rightarrow y
in
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
.
Since the restriction of a localization pair over
T_{x}
to
T_{y}
remains to be a localization pair,
the equivalence follows by the theorem.
∎

The rest of this section is devoted to proving the theorem.

1.12

For an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, we denote by
\mathrm{End}(\mathcal{C})
the
\infty
-category
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{C},\mathcal{C})
.
We have the morphism of endo-functors
\partial\xrightarrow{(-r_{0},r_{\infty})}\partial_{0}\oplus\partial_{\infty}=:\bm{\partial}
,
which we view as an edge of
\mathrm{End}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{fil}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})\bigr)
.
The diagram
\Lambda^{2}_{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{fil}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})\bigr)
depicted as

\mathrm{S}\circ\partial\rightarrow\partial\rightarrow\bm{\partial}
extends to a diagram

\Delta^{2}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{fil}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})\bigr)
in an essentially unique way, and we fix one.
Note that
\mathrm{End}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{fil}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})\bigr)
is stable by
[
HA
, 1.1.3.1]
.
We define an endo-functor on
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{fil}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})
to be

\Gamma:=\mathrm{cofib}\bigl(\mathrm{S}\circ\partial\rightarrow\partial\rightarrow\bm{\partial}\bigr).

This gives us a diagram
\Delta^{2}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{fil}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})\bigr)
depicted as

\partial\rightarrow\bm{\partial}\rightarrow\Gamma
.
Applying this functor to
\mathrm{id}\rightarrow\mathrm{S}
, we get a diagram

\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{2}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{fil}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})\bigr)

depicted as
(\partial\mathrm{S}\rightarrow\bm{\partial}\mathrm{S}\rightarrow\Gamma\mathrm{S})\rightarrow(\partial\rightarrow\bm{\partial}\rightarrow\Gamma)
.
We extend further to a
(\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{2})^{\triangleleft}
-shaped diagram by sending the cone point to
0
.
By composing with suitable maps
(\Delta^{1})^{3}\rightarrow(\Delta^{1}\times\Delta^{2})^{\triangleleft}
we have the diagrams

(\Delta^{1})^{3}\rightarrow\mathrm{End}\bigl(\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathrm{fil}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})\bigr)
depicted as

1.13

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
\infty
-category and assume we are given an endo-functor
F\colon\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
.
Let
X\in\mathcal{C}
and
\alpha\colon X\rightarrow F(X)
be a morphism.
Let
I^{\mathbb{N}}:=\Delta^{\{0,1\}}\cup\Delta^{\{1,2\}}\cup\dots\subset\Delta^{\mathbb{N}}
.
Then the edge
\alpha
induces the diagram
I^{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
by
\alpha\vee F(\alpha)\vee F^{2}(\alpha)\vee\dots
.
Since
I^{\mathbb{N}}\hookrightarrow\mathrm{N}(\mathbb{N})
is an inner anodyne, this diagram extends to a diagram

D\colon\mathrm{N}(\mathbb{N})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
.
We put
F^{\infty}_{\alpha}(X):=\mathop{\underrightarrow{\mathrm{lim}}}\nolimits(D)
, if it exists.
The following lemma will be used later.

Lemma
. —

Let
\mathcal{C}
be an
\infty
-category, and
F
be an endo-functor of
\mathcal{C}
.
For
X\in\mathcal{C}
, assume we are given a retract diagram
X\xrightarrow{i}F(X)\xrightarrow{r}X
.
Then this induces the retract diagram
X\xrightarrow{i}F^{\infty}(X)\xrightarrow{r}X
, if
F^{\infty}(X)
exists.

Proof.

Following
[
HTT
, 4.4.5]
, let
\mathrm{Ret}:=\Delta^{2}/\Delta^{\{0,2\}}
.
We construct diagrams
R_{n}\colon\mathrm{Ret}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
and
D_{n}\colon\mathrm{Ret}\times\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
for
n>0
inductively.
We put
R_{1}
to be the given retract diagram.
Assume we are given a retract diagram
X\xrightarrow{i_{n}}F^{n}(X)\xrightarrow{r_{n}}X
, denoted by
R_{n}
.
Let us construct
D_{n}
.
We have the map
(\Delta^{2}\times\{0\})\coprod_{\partial\Delta^{2}\times\{0\}}(\partial\Delta^{2}\times\Delta^{1})\rightarrow\mathcal{C}

sending
\Delta^{2}\times\{0\}
to the retract diagram
X\rightarrow F^{n}(X)\rightarrow X
,
and
\Delta^{\{0,2\}}\times\Delta^{1}
is sent to the point
\{X\}
,
and
\Delta^{\{0,1\}}\times\Delta^{1}
,
\Delta^{\{1,2\}}\times\Delta^{1}
are any homotopy commutative diagrams of the form below:

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{n}}
\textstyle{F^{n}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{F^{n}(i)}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{n+1}(X),}

\textstyle{F^{n}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{n}}
\scriptstyle{F^{n}(i)}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{n+1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{n}\circ F^{n}(r)}
\textstyle{X.}

By (the dual version of)
[
HTT
, 3.2.1.8]
, the diagram extends to a diagram

\Delta^{2}\times\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
.
By construction, this map factors through
\mathrm{Ret}\times\Delta^{1}
, and the induced map is

D_{n}\colon\mathrm{Ret}\times\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
.
This diagram can be depicted as

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{n}}
\textstyle{F^{n}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{r_{n}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{F^{n+1}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X,}

and the rows are retract diagrams.
Now, let
R_{n+1}:=D_{n}|_{\mathrm{Ret}\times\{1\}}
.
This inductive construction yields, for
n>0
, a diagram
D_{n}\colon\mathrm{Ret}\times\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
such that

D_{n}|_{\mathrm{Ret}\times\{0\}}=R_{n}
and
D_{n}|_{\mathrm{Ret}\times\{1\}}=R_{n+1}
.
By concatenating, we have
\mathrm{Ret}\times I^{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
such that
\mathrm{Ret}\times\{i\}=R_{i}
.
Since
I\hookrightarrow\mathbb{N}
is an inner anodyne, the diagram extends to
\mathrm{Ret}\times\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
by

[
HTT
, 2.3.2.4]
.
Taking the adjoint, we have the diagram
\mathbb{N}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{Ret},\mathcal{C})
, and take a colimit of this functor.
Since colimits can be computed pointwise by
[
HTT
, 5.1.2.3]
, the colimit diagram is the one we are seeking.
∎

Example
. —

Let
\mathcal{H}
be a reversible coefficient.
Then the rewind structure determines a retract to the canonical map
\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\Gamma^{\infty}_{r_{\infty}}\mathcal{H}
.
Indeed, by Lemma
Lemma
, it suffices to construct a retract of the map
r_{\infty}\colon\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\Gamma\mathcal{H}
.
Let
\phi\colon\Lambda^{2}_{0}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\mathcal{P},\mathcal{P})
be the diagram of functors
\partial_{0}\leftarrow\partial\rightarrow\partial_{\infty}
.
We have an equivalence
\Gamma\simeq\mathop{\underrightarrow{\mathrm{lim}}}\nolimits(\phi)
.
Regard the diagram (
1.7.i
) as a diagram of functors
R\colon\Delta^{2}\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}(\Lambda^{2}_{0},\mathcal{P})
,
and consider the composition
\mathop{\underrightarrow{\mathrm{lim}}}\nolimits\circ R
.
We have
(\mathop{\underrightarrow{\mathrm{lim}}}\nolimits\circ R)(\{1\})\simeq\Gamma\mathcal{H}
.
Moreover, since
\mathcal{H}
is
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariant, the morphism
q_{*}q^{*}\mathcal{H}\xrightarrow{r_{0}}\mathcal{H}
is an equivalence,
and
(\mathop{\underrightarrow{\mathrm{lim}}}\nolimits\circ R)(\Delta^{\{0,2\}})
is equivalent to the identity morphism of
\mathcal{H}
.

1.15

In this paragraph, we introduce the Suslin complex.
The construction is well-known to experts.
We let
\Delta^{n}_{\mathrm{geom}}:=S\times\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{Z}[x_{0},\dots,x_{n}]/(1-\sum x_{i}))
.
For
\phi\colon[m]\rightarrow[n]
in
\mathbf{\Delta}
,
we have the homomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{S}[x_{0},\dots,x_{n}]/(1-\sum x_{i})\rightarrow\mathcal{O}_{S}[y_{0},\dots,y_{m}]/(1-\sum y_{j})
sending
x_{i}
to
\sum_{j\in\phi^{-1}(i)}y_{j}
.
This gives us a cosimplicial scheme
\Delta^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{geom}}\colon\mathbf{\Delta}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
.

Now, let
\mathcal{P}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}^{\mathrm{op}}
be a coCartesian fibration such that the fiber over
T\in\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
is
equivalent to
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/T})

and the functor
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
that classifies the coCartesian fibration is
given by sending a morphism
f
in
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
to the pullback
f^{*}
.
For a rigorous construction of such coCartesian fibration, see
2.1
.
Since the pullback functor admits a right adjoint, the fibration is Cartesian as well by
[
HTT
, 5.2.2.5]
.
We put
p\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}:=\mathcal{P}\times_{\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}^{\mathrm{op}},\Delta^{\bullet}_{\mathrm{geom}}}\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
.
Let
D\colon\Delta^{1}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}
be the unique map such that

D|_{\Delta^{\{0\}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}=[0]
and
D|_{\Delta^{\{1\}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}=\mathrm{id}
.
We define the
Suslin complex functor
by the composition

\displaystyle\mathrm{Sus}^{\mathcal{D}}_{\bullet}\colon\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}([0],\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{LKE}}\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}})\xrightarrow[(\star)]{\mathrm{RKE}}\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}(D,\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}})

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}}(\Delta^{\{0\}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}})\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}},\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{D}}(\mathrm{Sch}_{/S})),

where
\mathrm{LKE}
is the
p
-left Kan extension functor and

(\star)
is the
p
-right Kan extension functor along
\Delta^{\{1\}}\times\mathbf{\Delta}^{\mathrm{op}}\hookrightarrow D
.
Informally,
\mathrm{Sus}^{\mathcal{D}}_{\bullet}(\mathcal{F})
is the simplicial diagram of
\infty
-presheaves

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.75pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&&&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.75pt\raise
