3.8

Finally, we establish some functorialities of the microlocalized map.
We consider 4 types of functorialities: the functoriality when we change the base data, when we change the support systems,
when we change the base, and when we change the schemes.

Functoriality for the change of base data:

Assume we are given a morphism
m\colon(X,E^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},\mathrm{d}_{\nu^{\prime}})\rightarrow(X,E,\nu,\mathrm{d}_{\nu})

of base data with enough frames, and a presheaf with support structure
(\mathscr{S},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
with repsect to
(X,E,\nu,\mathrm{d}_{\nu})
.
Abusing the notation, we also denote by
m\colon E^{\prime}\rightarrow E
the associated morphism.
We define
\mathscr{S}\otimes\nu^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
by the composition

m^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{S}\otimes\nu^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}\otimes\nu\xrightarrow{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}\partial\mathscr{S}.

This defines a support structure.
Let
C\subset E
be a conic.
By construction, we have the homomorphism
\Gamma(X,\mathscr{S})_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime},C}\rightarrow\Gamma(X,\mathscr{S})_{m^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\prime},m^{-1}(C)}
.
Also by construction, we have the following diagram
\Delta^{2}\times\Delta^{1}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
depicted as

\textstyle{\Gamma(X,\mathscr{S})_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime},C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}(E,\omega_{X/S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}(E\setminus C,\omega_{X/S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,\mathscr{S})_{m^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\prime},m^{-1}(C)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}(E^{\prime},\omega_{X/S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}(E^{\prime}\setminus m^{-1}(C),\omega_{X/S})}

This diagram induces the homotopy commutative diagram

\textstyle{\Gamma(X,\mathscr{S})_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime},C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{C}(E,\omega_{X/S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,\mathscr{S})_{m^{*}\mathcal{E}^{\prime},m^{-1}(C)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{m^{-1}(C)}(E^{\prime},\omega_{X/S}).}

Functoriality for the change of presheaves:

Let us fix base data
(X,E,\nu,\mathrm{d}_{\nu})
, and assume we are given a homomorphism of presheaves

\phi\colon\mathscr{S}_{0}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{1}
endowed with support structures
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{0}
,
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{1}

such that the following diagram on the left commutes.
Similarly to the previous functoriality, we may construct the following homotopy commutative diagram on the right below.
The detail is left to the reader.

\textstyle{\mathscr{S}_{0}\otimes\nu\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\phi\otimes\mathrm{id}}
\textstyle{\partial\mathscr{S}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\partial\phi}
\textstyle{\mathscr{S}_{1}\otimes\nu\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{1}}
\textstyle{\partial\mathscr{S}_{1},}

\textstyle{\Gamma(X,\mathscr{S}_{0})_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{C}(E,\omega_{X/S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X,\mathscr{S}_{1})_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{C}(E,\omega_{X/S}).}

Functoriality for the change of base:

Assume we are given a
smooth
morphism
g\colon S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
.
Since any smooth scheme over
S^{\prime}
is smooth over
S
as well, we have the homomorphism

\Gamma(X,\mathscr{S})_{C}\rightarrow\Gamma(X_{S^{\prime}},g^{*}\mathscr{S})_{C_{S^{\prime}}}
for any conic
C\subset E
,
and we have the following homotopy commutative diagram, whose detailed construction is left to the reader:

\textstyle{\Gamma(X,\mathscr{S})_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{C}(E,\omega_{X/S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(X_{S^{\prime}},g^{*}\mathscr{S})_{C_{S^{\prime}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{C_{S^{\prime}}}(E_{S^{\prime}},\omega_{X_{S^{\prime}}/S^{\prime}}).}

Functoriality for the change of schemes:

For the last functoriality, assume we are given a proper morphism
h\colon X\rightarrow Y
over
S
.
We assume given base data
(Y,E,\nu,\mathrm{d}_{\nu})
.
This defines a base data
(X,E\times_{Y}X,h^{-1}\nu,\mathrm{d}_{h^{-1}\nu})
, and let
(\mathscr{S},\mathcal{E}^{\prime})

be a presheaf with support structure with respect to this base data.
Let
g\colon E\times_{Y}X\rightarrow E
be the induced proper morphism.
The morphism

h_{*}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\colon h_{*}\mathscr{S}\otimes\nu\cong h_{*}(\mathscr{S}\otimes h^{-1}\nu)\rightarrow h_{*}\partial\mathscr{S}\cong\partial h_{*}\mathscr{S}

defines a presheaf with support structure
(h_{*}\mathscr{S},h_{*}\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
.
For
V\rightarrow T
in
(Y/S)_{\mathrm{zar}}
, by definition, we have the following commutative diagram:

\textstyle{\Gamma(V\times_{Y}X/T,\mathscr{S}\otimes h^{-1}\nu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Gamma(V\times_{Y}X/T,\partial\mathscr{S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\sim}
\textstyle{\Gamma(V/T,h_{*}\mathscr{S}\otimes\nu)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\Gamma(V/T,\partial h_{*}\mathscr{S}).}

Let
C\subset E\times_{Y}X
be a conic.
This commutative diagram defines a homomorphism
\Gamma(X,\mathscr{S})_{C}\rightarrow\Gamma(Y,h_{*}\mathscr{S})_{g(C)}
.
Using this, we have the following homotopy commutative diagram, whose detailed construction is left to the reader

\textstyle{\Gamma(X,\mathscr{S})_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{C}(E\times_{Y}X,\omega_{X/S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Gamma(Y,h_{*}\mathscr{S})_{g(C)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{g(C)}(E,\omega_{Y/S}).}

4
Preliminary — Nearby cycles and flat étale systems

The rest of this paper is devoted mainly to construct a localization system.
Our localization system is constructed from étale sheaves.
In this section, all the schemes are over the field
k
of positive characteristic,
and
\Lambda
be a commutative finite local ring in which
\mathrm{char}(k)\neq 0
is invertible.
For a scheme
X
, the derived (
1
-)category of étale
\Lambda
-modules is denoted by
D(X,\Lambda)
or
D(X)
.
We denote by
D_{\mathrm{c}}(X)
(resp.
D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(X)
) be the full subcategory of
D(X)

consisting of constructible (resp. constructible and finite Tor-dimensional) complexes.
When
X
is quasi-compact, note that
D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(X)=D^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{ctf}}(X)
.

4.1

A
geometric point

s
of a scheme
S
is a morphism
p\colon s\rightarrow S
where
s
is a spectrum of a separably closed field
k(s)
.
We abbreviate the geometric point
p
by
s
if no confusion may arise.
If we are given another geometric point
p^{\prime}\colon s^{\prime}\rightarrow S
, a morphism of geometric points
p\rightarrow p^{\prime}
is nothing but an
S
-morphism
s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
.
The geometric point
s
is said to be
algebraic
if the extension of fields
k(s)/k(p(s))
is algebraic.
We denote by
S_{(s)}
, or more precisely
S_{(p)}
, the strict henselization of
S
at
s
.
A
specialization map

\phi
of geometric points, denoted by
\phi\colon t\rightsquigarrow s
, is a morphism of
S
-schemes
t\rightarrow S_{(s)}
.
Let
f\colon S\rightarrow T
be a morphism.
We denote the geometric point
s\rightarrow S\xrightarrow{f}T
of
T
by
f(s)
.
A specialization map
t\rightsquigarrow s
on
S
induces the specialization map
f(t)\rightsquigarrow f(s)
on
T
.

4.2

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow S
be a morphism of finite type between noetherian schemes.
For a geometric point
s\rightarrow S
, we put
X_{(s)}:=X\times_{S}S_{(s)}
.
Assume we are given a specialization map
\phi\colon t\rightsquigarrow s
of geometric points on
S
.
Consider the Cartesian diagram

\textstyle{X_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{X_{(s)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\square}
\textstyle{X_{t}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{s\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{(s)}}
\textstyle{t\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}

Then we define

\Psi_{f,\phi}:=i^{*}\circ\mathrm{R}j_{*}\colon D^{+}(X_{t})\rightarrow D^{+}(X_{s}).

We often denote
\Psi_{f,\phi}
simply by
\Psi_{\phi}
or
\Psi_{s\leftarrow t}
if no confusion may arise.

Remark
. —

1.

The functor
\Psi_{f,\phi}
induces a functor
D^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{t},\Lambda)\rightarrow D^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{s},\Lambda)
.
Indeed, since
S
is quasi-compact, the dimension of the fibers of
f
is bounded by [EGA IV, 13.1.7].
If
t
is algebraic, then
\Psi_{\phi}
preserves the boundedness by
[
O
, Prop 3.1]
(or its evident analogue for our
\Psi_{\phi}
).
Let
\alpha\colon s\rightarrow s^{\prime}
be a morphism of geometric points of
S
.
To treat the general case, it suffices to show that for
\mathcal{F}\in D^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{s})
, the pushforward
\mathrm{R}\alpha_{*}\mathcal{F}
is bounded.
We may assume that
\mathcal{F}
is a sheaf.
We have
\dim(X_{s})\leq N
since
X_{s}
is of finite type over
s
.
For an open affine subscheme
U\subset X_{s^{\prime}}
,
U\times_{X_{s^{\prime}}}X_{s}
is affine as well, and [SGA 4, Exp. XIV, Cor 3.2] implies that

\mathrm{H}^{i}(U\times_{X_{s^{\prime}}}X_{s},\mathcal{F})=0
for any
i>N
. Thus
\mathrm{R}^{i}\alpha_{*}\mathcal{F}=0
for
i>N
.
Furthermore, by the same argument as
[
O
, Rem 8.3]
using [SGA 4, Exp. XVII, 5.2.11],

\Psi_{\phi}
preserves finite Tor-dimensional complexes as well.

2.

Let
x
be a geometric point of
X_{s}
.
This yields a point
(\phi,x)
of the topos
X\overleftarrow{\times}_{S}S
.
For
\mathcal{F}\in D^{+}(X)
, we may consider the nearby cycle functor over general base

\mathrm{R}\Psi_{f}(\mathcal{F})\in D^{+}(X\overleftarrow{\times}_{S}S)
(cf.
[
I
, 1.2]
).
If
t
is a geometric generic point, then we have
\mathrm{R}\Psi_{f}(\mathcal{F})_{(\phi,x)}\xrightarrow{\sim}\Psi_{f,\phi}(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{x}
.
In general, these are different since
\mathrm{R}\Psi_{f}(\mathcal{F})_{(\phi,x)}
is the cohomology of “Milnor tube”
whereas
\Psi_{f,\phi}(\mathcal{F})_{x}
is that of “Milnor fiber”.
However, if
(f,\mathcal{F})
is
\Psi
-good in the sense of Illusie
[
I
, 1.5]
(7)
(7)

(7)

The notion first appeared in
[
O
]
as far as the author knows, even though Orgogozo did not give a name.
,
these coincide for any geometric point
t
.

4.3 Definition
. —

A complex
\mathcal{F}\in D^{+}(X_{t})
is said to be
f
-good
(
at
t
)
if for any morphism
g\colon S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
, any specialization map

\phi^{\prime}\colon t^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow s^{\prime}
on
S^{\prime}
, and a morphism of geometric points
h\colon g(t^{\prime})\rightarrow t
, the canonical morphism

\Psi_{f,g(\phi^{\prime})}\bigl(h^{*}\mathcal{F}\bigr)\rightarrow\Psi_{f^{\prime},\phi^{\prime}}\bigl(h^{*}\mathcal{F}\bigr),

where
f^{\prime}
is the base change
f_{S^{\prime}}=f\times_{S}S^{\prime}
,
is an isomorphism in
D^{+}(X_{s^{\prime}})
.
A complex
\mathcal{G}\in D^{+}(X)
is said to be
f
-good
if for any geometric point
t
of
S
,
the pullback
\mathcal{G}_{t}\in D^{+}(X_{t})
is
f
-good.

Example
. —

For
\mathcal{F}\in D^{+}(X)
, assume that
(f,\mathcal{F})
is
\Psi
-good in the sense of Illusie.
Then
\mathcal{F}
is
f
-good in the sense above.
Thus, we have the following examples of
f
-good complexes:

1.

If
\mathcal{F}
is universally locally acyclic over
S
, then
\mathcal{F}
is
f
-good by
[
I
, 1.7 (b)]
.

2.

Let
\mathcal{F}\in D^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X,\Lambda)
, and assume that there exists a subscheme
Z\subset X
such that
the composition
Z\rightarrow S
is quasi-finite and
\mathcal{F}|_{X\setminus Z}
is universally locally acyclic relative to
f^{\prime}
,
where
f^{\prime}\colon X\setminus Z\rightarrow S
.
Then
\mathcal{F}
is
f
-good by
[
I
, 1.7 (c)]
.

In fact, Gabber showed that
f
-good complexes are
\Psi
-good in the sense of Illusie,
but we do not use this result in this paper.

4.4

The following corollary of Orgogozo’s theorem is fundamental to us:

Corollary

(of Orgogozo’s theorem)
. —

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow S
be a morphism of finite type between noetherian schemes,

u
be a geometric point of
S
,
\phi\colon u\rightsquigarrow t
be a specialization map on
S
,
and
\mathcal{F}
be an object of
D^{\mathrm{b}}_{\mathrm{c}}(X_{u})
.

1.

Assume that
u
is algebraic.
There exists a modification
g\colon S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
such that, for any geometric point
u^{\prime}
of
S^{\prime}
with
g(u^{\prime})=u
,
the complex
\mathcal{F}
considered as an object of
D^{+}(X_{u^{\prime}})
is
f_{S^{\prime}}
-good.

2.

If
\mathcal{F}
is
f
-good and belongs to
D^{+}_{\mathrm{c}}(X_{u})

(
resp. to
D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(X_{u})
)
,
the complex
\Psi_{f,\phi}(\mathcal{F})
belongs to
D^{+}_{\mathrm{c}}(X_{t})

(
resp. to
D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(X_{t})
)
.

Proof.

Let us show the first claim.
By
[
O
, Lem 4.3]
, we may assume that
u
is a geometric
generic
point.
Since
\mathcal{F}
is bounded, we may assume that
\mathcal{F}
is a
\Lambda
-module.
Since
u
is algebraic and
\mathcal{F}
is bounded and constructible,
there exists a finite surjective morphism
S_{0}\rightarrow S
and a constructible
\Lambda
-module
\mathcal{G}

on
X_{S_{0}}
whose pullback
\mathcal{G}_{u}
coincides with
\mathcal{F}
.
There exists a modification
S^{\prime}_{0}\rightarrow S_{0}
over which
\mathcal{G}
becomes good by Orgogozo’s theorem
[
O
, Thm 2.1]
.
By
[
O
, Lem 3.2]
, we can take a following diagram

\textstyle{T\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{fin\ surj}}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{alt}}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{modif}}
\textstyle{S.}

Then
S^{\prime}
is what we need.
Indeed, any specialization map
u\rightsquigarrow s^{\prime}
on
S^{\prime}
can be lifted to that on
T
since the morphism
T\rightarrow S^{\prime}
is finite surjective,
and we have
\Psi_{f_{T},s^{\prime}\leftarrow u}\cong\Psi_{f_{S^{\prime}},s^{\prime}\leftarrow u}
since the morphism
T\rightarrow S^{\prime}
is finite.

Let us show the second claim.
We may assume that
u
is a geometric generic point.
Even if
u
is not algebraic, we can still take a proper surjection
S_{0}\rightarrow S
and
\mathcal{G}
as above.
By
[
O
, Thm 8.1]
, we can take a modification
T\rightarrow S_{0}
such that
\mathcal{G}_{T}
is
f_{T}
-good and

\mathrm{R}\Psi_{f_{T}}(\mathcal{G}_{T})\in D^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{T}\overleftarrow{\times}_{T}T)
is constructible.
Since
T\rightarrow S
is proper surjective, we can take a lifting of
\phi
on
T
.
Using this lifting,
\phi
can be viewed as a point of the topos
T\overleftarrow{\times}_{T}T
,
and yields a morphism
\sigma_{\phi}\colon X_{t}\rightarrow X_{T}\times_{T}(T\overleftarrow{\times}_{T}T)\cong X_{T}\overleftarrow{\times}_{T}T
,
where the product is taken in the category of topoi.
Since
\mathcal{F}
is assumed
f
-good,
\sigma_{\phi}^{*}\mathrm{R}\Psi_{f_{T}}(\mathcal{G}_{T})
is isomorphic to
\Psi_{f,\phi}(\mathcal{F})
,
and the constructibility follows.
The claim for the finite Tor-dimensionality follows by Remark
Remark
.
1
.
∎

4.5

A certain transitivity of nearby cycle functor is a key to Saito’s construction of characteristic cycle,
and this appears rather implicitly in
[
S1
, Prop 2.8]
.
This is also the case for our construction, and we use the following properties of nearby cycle functor due to Gabber:

Theorem

(Gabber)
. —

Let
f\colon X\rightarrow S
be a morphism of finite type between noetherian schemes,

\eta
be a geometric point of
S
, and
\mathcal{F}\in D^{+}(X_{\eta})
.
Let
\phi\colon\eta\rightsquigarrow\xi
,
\psi\colon\xi\rightsquigarrow s
be specialization maps.
If
\mathcal{F}
is
f
-good, the following hold.

1.

The complex
\Psi_{f,\phi}(\mathcal{F})
in
D^{+}(X_{\xi})
is
f
-good.

2.

The canonical homomorphism
\Psi_{f,\psi\circ\phi}(\mathcal{F})\rightarrow(\Psi_{f,\psi}\circ\Psi_{f,\phi})(\mathcal{F})
is an isomorphism.

4.6

Let
S
be a scheme.
Let
s\in S
be a point.
We often denote by
\overline{s}
an algebraic geometric point over
s
.
Now, we put
\mathrm{Cons}(S):=D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(S,\Lambda)
.
Let
\mathrm{Cons}_{d}(S)
be the full subcategory of
D_{\mathrm{ctf}}(S,\Lambda)
consisting of complexes
\mathcal{F}

such that
\mathrm{Supp}(\mathcal{H}^{i}\mathcal{F})
is of dimension
\leq d
for any
i
.
Since the pullback is exact and by considering the localization sequence, the morphism
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}_{d}(S)\rightarrow\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(S)
is injective.

Now, let
u\rightsquigarrow s
is a specialization map on
S
.
Since
\Psi_{s\leftarrow u}
is a functor
\mathrm{Cons}(X_{u})\rightarrow D^{\mathrm{b}}(X_{s})

and may not factor through
\mathrm{Cons}(X_{s})
, it does not induce a homomorphism

\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{u})\rightarrow\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{s})
.
However, we have the following:

Lemma
. —

Let
X\rightarrow S
be a morphism of finite type between noetherian schemes, and let
u\rightsquigarrow s
be a specialization map of geometric points of
S
.
Let
\mathcal{F}_{+}
,
\mathcal{F}_{-}
,
\mathcal{G}_{+}
,
\mathcal{G}_{-}
be good objects in
\mathrm{Cons}(X_{u})
over
S
.
Assume
[\mathcal{F}_{+}]-[\mathcal{F}_{-}]=[\mathcal{G}_{+}]-[\mathcal{G}_{-}]
in
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{u})
.
Then for any specialization map
u\rightsquigarrow s
, we have the following in
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{s})

[\Psi_{s\leftarrow u}(\mathcal{F}_{+})]-[\Psi_{s\leftarrow u}(\mathcal{F}_{-})]=[\Psi_{s\leftarrow u}(\mathcal{G}_{+})]-[\Psi_{s\leftarrow u}(\mathcal{G}_{-})].

Proof.

By replacing
S
by the closure of the image of
u
in
S
, we may assume that
u
is a geometric generic point of
S
.
By definition, there exist finitely many exact triangles

\{\mathcal{H}^{(k)}_{0}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{(k)}_{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{(k)}_{2}\}_{k\in K\sqcup K^{\prime}}
in
\mathrm{Cons}(X_{u})
such that

[\mathcal{F}_{+}]-[\mathcal{G}_{+}]-[\mathcal{F}_{-}]+[\mathcal{G}_{-}]=\sum_{k\in K}\bigl([\mathcal{H}_{1}^{(k)}]-[\mathcal{H}_{0}^{(k)}]-[\mathcal{H}_{2}^{(k)}]\bigr)-\sum_{k\in K^{\prime}}\bigl([\mathcal{H}_{1}^{(k)}]-[\mathcal{H}_{0}^{(k)}]-[\mathcal{H}_{2}^{(k)}]\bigr)

in the free group generated by objects of
\mathrm{Cons}(X_{u})
.
We can take a modification
g\colon S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
over which
\mathcal{H}^{(k)}_{i}
are good for any
i=0,1,2
and
k\in K\sqcup K^{\prime}
.
Because
\mathcal{H}^{(k)}_{i}
are good,
\Psi_{s^{\prime}\leftarrow u}(\mathcal{H}_{i}^{(k)})

are constructible for any specialization map
u\rightsquigarrow s^{\prime}
on
S^{\prime}
.
Thus, we can check the equality in
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{s^{\prime}})
for the specialization map
u\rightsquigarrow s^{\prime}
easily.
We may find a specialization
u\rightsquigarrow s^{\prime}
in
S^{\prime}
such that
s^{\prime}
sits over
s
and the extension
k(s^{\prime})=k(s)
.
Because
\mathcal{F}_{\pm}
is good,
\Psi_{s\leftarrow u}(\mathcal{F}_{\pm})=\Psi_{s^{\prime}\leftarrow u}(g^{*}\mathcal{F}_{\pm})

in
\mathrm{Cons}(X_{s^{\prime}})=\mathrm{Cons}(X_{s})
, and similarly for
\mathcal{G}_{\pm}
.
Thus, the claim follows.
∎

Definition
. —

Let
h\colon X\rightarrow S
be a morphism of finite type between noetherian schemes,
and let
u
be a geometric point of
S
over
s\in S
.

1.

Let
L/K
be an extension of fields, and
Y\rightarrow\mathrm{Spec}(K)
be a scheme.
We say that
F\in\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{L})
is
descendable to
K
if for any
K
-automorphism

\sigma\colon\mathrm{Spec}(L)\rightarrow\mathrm{Spec}(L)
, we have
\sigma^{*}F=F
.
The subgroup of
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}_{(d)}(X_{L})
generated by descendable elements to
K
is denoted by

\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}_{(d)}^{\mathrm{dec}}(X_{L},K)
.

2.

An element
F\in\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}({X_{u}})
is said to be
h
-good

if there exists a presentation
F=\sum n_{i}[\mathcal{F}_{i}]
such that
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is
h
-good for any
i
.
The subgroup of
h
-good elements of
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{u})
is denoted by
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}^{\mathrm{gd}}(X_{u},h)
.

Let
F
be a good element as in
2
.
The above lemma implies that if we are given a specialization
u\rightsquigarrow t
,
the element
\sum n_{i}[\Psi_{t\leftarrow u}(\mathcal{F}_{i})]
does not depend on the choice of the presentations.
We denote this by
\Psi_{t\leftarrow u}(F)
, which belongs to
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}^{\mathrm{gd}}(X_{t},h)
.
Namely, we have a homomorphism
\Psi_{t\leftarrow u}\colon\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}^{\mathrm{gd}}(X_{u},h)\rightarrow\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}^{\mathrm{gd}}(X_{t},h)
.

Example
. —

Let
\mathcal{F}
be a descendable element in
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{\overline{\eta}})
, and
\mathcal{L}
be an object of
\mathrm{Cons}(X_{\eta})
.
Then
[\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\overline{\eta}}]
in
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{\overline{\eta}})
,
where
\mathcal{L}_{\overline{\eta}}
denotes the pullback to
X_{\overline{\eta}}
, is descendable,
since
\mathcal{L}_{\overline{\eta}}
is
\pi_{1}(\eta)
-equivalent.
This element is denoted by
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{L}
.

4.7

Now, here is one of the main players of this paper:

Definition
. —

Let
S^{0}
be the set of generic points of
S
,
and fix
algebraic
geometric points
\{\overline{\eta}\}_{\eta\in S^{0}}
.
A collection
F:=\{F_{\eta}\}_{\eta\in S^{0}}
in
\prod_{\eta\in S^{0}}\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}^{\mathrm{gd}}(X_{\overline{\eta}},h)

is said to be a
flat étale system
if for any algebraic geometric point
\overline{s}\rightarrow S
,
and specialization maps
\overline{\eta}\rightsquigarrow\overline{s}
,

\overline{\eta}^{\prime}\rightsquigarrow\overline{s}
such that
\eta,\eta^{\prime}\in S^{0}
, we have an equality

\Psi_{\overline{s}\leftarrow\overline{\eta}}(F_{\eta})=\Psi_{\overline{s}\leftarrow\overline{\eta}^{\prime}}(F_{\eta^{\prime}})
.
The set of flat étale systems forms a subgroup, and this group is denoted by
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}(X/S,\{\overline{\eta}\})
.
We also define the subgroup of
flat étale system of dimension
\leq d
by

\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{d}(X/S,\{\overline{\eta}\})=\prod_{\eta\in S^{0}}\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}_{d}(X_{\overline{\eta}})\cap\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}(X/S,\{\overline{\eta}\}),

where the intersection is taken in
\prod_{\eta}\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{\overline{\eta}})
.

Lemma
. —

Assume we are given sets of algebraic geometric points

E=\{\overline{\eta}\}_{\eta\in S^{0}}
and
E^{\prime}=\{\overline{\eta}^{\prime}\}_{\eta\in S^{0}}
.
Then there exists a canonical isomorphism
p_{E^{\prime},E}\colon\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}(X/S,E)\rightarrow\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}(X/S,E^{\prime})
such that if we are given another set
E^{\prime\prime}
, we have

\epsilon_{E^{\prime\prime},E^{\prime}}\circ\epsilon_{E^{\prime},E}=\epsilon_{E^{\prime\prime},E}
and
\epsilon_{E,E}=\mathrm{id}
.
Thus,
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}(X/S,E)
does not depend on the choice of
E
, and we denote this simply by
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}(X/S)
, and similarly for
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{d}(X/S)
.

Proof.

Let
F=\{F_{\eta}\}
be an element of
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}(X/S,E)
.
Assume we are given an
S
-automorphism
\overline{\eta}\rightarrow\overline{\eta}
.
This yields a specialization map
\overline{\eta}\rightsquigarrow\overline{\eta}
,
and the compatibility condition implies that
F_{\eta}
is in fact descendable to
\eta
.
A morphism
E^{\prime}\rightarrow E
is a collection of
S
-isomorphisms
\overline{\eta}^{\prime}\rightarrow\overline{\eta}
.
For a morphism
\phi=\{\phi_{\eta}\}\colon E^{\prime}\rightarrow E
, we may consider the pullback
\phi^{*}\colon\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}(X/S,E)\rightarrow\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}(X/S,E^{\prime})
.
Assume we are given another morphism
\phi^{\prime}=\{\phi^{\prime}_{\eta}\}
.
Then we can find
\sigma\colon\overline{\eta}\rightarrow\overline{\eta}
over
\eta
such that
\phi^{\prime}_{\eta}=\sigma\circ\phi
.
Since
F
is descendable, we have
\phi^{*}(F)=\phi^{\prime*}(F)
, and we are allowed to define
\epsilon_{E^{\prime},E}:=\phi^{*}
.
It is easy to check the required properties of
\epsilon
.
∎

Assume we are given a specialization map
\eta\rightsquigarrow s
on
S
.
To ease the notation, from now on, we sometimes abbreviate
\Psi_{\overline{s}\leftarrow\overline{\eta}}
by
\Psi_{s\leftarrow\eta}
.

4.8 Lemma
. —

Let
X\rightarrow S
be a morphism of finite type between noetherian schemes.

1.

Let
g\colon S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
be a morphism between noetherian scheme,
and
F
be a flat étale system on
X
over
S
of dimension
\leq d
.
For each
\eta^{\prime}\in S^{\prime 0}
, we can take a specialization map
\eta\rightsquigarrow g(\eta^{\prime})
where
\eta
is some generic point of
S^{0}
,
and we may consider

\bigl\{\Psi_{g(\eta^{\prime})\leftarrow\eta}(F)\bigr\}_{\eta^{\prime}\in S^{\prime 0}}\in\prod_{\eta^{\prime}\in S^{\prime 0}}\mathrm{K}_{0}(\mathrm{Cons}(X_{S^{\prime},\overline{\eta}^{\prime}}))
.
Then this does not depend on the choice of the specialization map and it belongs to
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{d}(X_{S^{\prime}}/S^{\prime})
.
This flat étale system is called the pullback of
F
by
g
, and denoted by
g^{*}(F)
.
The pullback is transitive.

2.

Assume that
S
is excellent.
For each generic point
\eta\in S^{0}
, assume we are given
F_{\eta}\in\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}^{\mathrm{dec}}_{d}(X_{\overline{\eta}},\eta)
.
Then there exists a modification
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
and
\{G_{\xi}\}_{\xi\in S^{\prime 0}}\in\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{d}(X_{S^{\prime}}/S^{\prime})
such that
F_{\xi}=G_{\xi}

in
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{\overline{\xi}})
for any
\xi\in S^{\prime 0}\xrightarrow{\sim}S^{0}
.

Proof.

Let us show the first claim.
First,
\Psi_{g(\eta^{\prime})\leftarrow\eta}(F)
is good by Theorem
4.5
.
The compatibility condition can be checked by the definition of goodness,
and the last transitivity follows by Theorem
4.5
.
It remains to show that it is of dimension
\leq d
.
For a specialization map
\eta\rightsquigarrow g(\eta^{\prime})
as in the statement,
we may find a discrete valuation ring
R
with residue field
k
and a commutative diagram

\textstyle{v:=\mathrm{Spec}(k)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V:=\mathrm{Spec}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S}

such that
v
is sent to
\eta^{\prime}
, and the generic point of
\mathrm{Spec}(R)
is sent to
\eta

(
e.g.
blowup
S
at the closure of
g(\eta^{\prime})
,
and take an extension of discrete valuation ring corresponding to the generic point of the exceptional divisor).
Let
\eta_{V}
be the generic point of
V
.
Since
F
is good, it is compatible with base change, and it suffices to show the claim for
S^{\prime}=v
,
S=V
.
Now, we may assume that
F=[\mathcal{F}]
for
\mathcal{F}\in\mathrm{Cons}_{d}(X_{\overline{\eta}})
.
Since
\mathrm{Supp}(\Psi_{v\leftarrow\eta_{V}}(\mathcal{F}))\subset(\overline{\mathrm{Supp}(\mathcal{F})})_{v}
,
and there exists a scheme structure on
\overline{\mathrm{Supp}(\mathcal{F})}
which is flat over
R
by [EGA IV, 2.8.5],
the claim follows.

Let us show the second claim.
By considering the modification
\coprod_{\eta\in S^{0}}\overline{S}_{\eta}\rightarrow S
,
we may assume
S
is irreducible.
Write
F_{\eta}=\sum n_{i}[\mathcal{F}_{i}]
where
\eta
is the generic point of
S
.
By modifying
S
, we may assume that
\mathcal{F}_{i}
is good over
S
for any
i
by Corollary
Corollary
.
Further modifying
S
, we may assume that
S
is normal since
S
is assumed to be excellent,
in which case
F_{\eta}
is a flat étale system.
Indeed, let
\overline{s}\rightarrow S
be a geometric point over a point
s\in S
, and assume we are given specialization maps

\phi,\phi^{\prime}\colon\overline{\eta}\rightsquigarrow\overline{s}
.
We wish to show that the nearby cycles along
\phi
and
\phi^{\prime}
are the same.
Since étale topos is insensitive to inseparable extensions, we may assume that
\overline{\eta}
is a spectrum of an algebraically closed field.
Now, giving
\phi
,
\phi^{\prime}
is equivalent to giving morphisms
\widetilde{\phi},\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime}\colon\overline{\eta}\rightarrow S_{(\overline{s})}
.
Since
S
is geometrically unibranch,
S_{(\overline{s})}
is irreducible.
Let
\xi_{s}
be its generic point.
Then
\widetilde{\phi}
,
\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime}
induce
\psi,\psi^{\prime}\colon\overline{\eta}\rightarrow\xi_{s}
.
By uniqueness of algebraic closure, we may find
\sigma
rendering the following diagram commutative:

\textstyle{\overline{\eta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\xi_{s}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{S_{(\overline{s})}.}
\textstyle{\overline{\eta}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\widetilde{\phi}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{\prime}}

Since
F_{\eta}
is descendable, this implies that
\Psi_{\phi}(F)=\Psi_{\phi^{\prime}\circ\sigma}(F)=\Psi_{\phi^{\prime}}(\sigma_{*}F)=\Psi_{\phi^{\prime}}(F)

(where
\sigma_{*}:=(\sigma^{*})^{-1}
), and
F_{\eta}
is a flat étale system as required.
∎

Definition
. —

Let
X\rightarrow S
be a morphism of finite type between noetherian schemes.
By the lemma above, the assignment
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{d}(X_{T}/T)
to
T\in\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
defines a presheaf on
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
.
This presheaf is denoted by
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{d,X/S}
, and
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X/S}=\bigcup_{d\geq 0}\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{d,X/S}
.

Remark
. —

Let
\mathrm{geom}(S)
be the set of geometric points of
S
.
Let
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{\mathrm{TS}}(X/S)
be the subgroup of
\prod_{s\in\mathrm{geom}(S)}\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}^{\mathrm{gd}}(X_{s},h)

consisting of elements
\{F_{s}\}
such that for any specialization map
t\rightsquigarrow s
,
we have
\Psi_{s\leftarrow t}(F_{t})=F_{s}
.
In view of the lemmas above, the restriction homomorphism
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{\mathrm{TS}}(X/S)\rightarrow\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}(X/S)
is an isomorphism.
The description of
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{\mathrm{TS}}
is closer to the spirit of the flat function in
[
S1
, Def 2.1]
,
and illustrates the terminology “flat étale system” more clearly.
On the other hand,
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}
is closer to the spirit of the relative cycles in
[
SV
]
, and has an advantage in its brevity for constructing elements,
which is why we employed
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}
rather than
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{\mathrm{TS}}
.

4.9

Let
K
be a field of characteristic
p>0
.
Since
\Lambda
is assumed to a local ring, we have the unique homomorphism

\dim_{\Lambda}\colon\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}\bigl(\mathrm{Spec}(\overline{K})\bigr)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbb{Z}
,
called the
dimension homomorphism
(or
rank homomorphism
), such that
[\Lambda]
is sent to
1
.
We sometimes denote
\dim_{\Lambda}
by
\dim
.
Let
Y\rightarrow\mathrm{Spec}(K)
be a morphism of finite type.
We define a homomorphism

d_{Y}\colon\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}^{\mathrm{des}}_{0}(Y_{\overline{K}},K)\rightarrow Z_{0}(Y)[1/p];\quad\mathcal{G}\mapsto\sum_{s\in|Y|}\frac{\dim_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{G}_{\overline{s}})}{[k(s):K]_{\mathrm{insep}}}\cdot[s],

where
[,]_{\mathrm{insep}}
denotes the inseparable degree of the extension
of fields and
\overline{s}
is any geometric point of
Y_{\overline{K}}
lying above
s
.
Since
\mathcal{G}
is descendable,
\dim_{\Lambda}(\mathcal{G}_{\overline{s}})
does not depend on the choice of
\overline{s}
,
and the homomorphism is well-defined.
Recall conventions from
2.6
.

Lemma
. —

Let
h\colon X\rightarrow S
be a morphism of finite type between noetherian
k
-schemes, and
p:=\mathrm{char}(k)>0
.
The composition

d_{h}\colon\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{0}(X/S)\rightarrow\prod_{\eta\in S^{0}}\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}^{\mathrm{des}}_{0}(X_{\overline{\eta}},\eta)\xrightarrow{\prod d_{X_{\eta}}}\prod_{\eta\in S^{0}}Z_{0}(X_{\eta})[1/p]

factors through
z(X/S,0)[1/p]
, and defines a homomorphism
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{0}(X/S)\rightarrow z(X/S,0)[1/p]
.
Moreover, if we are given a morphism
g\colon S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
between noetherian schemes,
the following diagram of abelian groups commutes, where
\mathrm{cycl}(g)
denotes the pullback homomorphism for the relative cycle group:

\textstyle{\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{0}(X/S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{h}}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}}
\textstyle{z(X/S,0)[1/p]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{cycl}(g)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{0}(X_{S^{\prime}}/S^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{h^{\prime}}}
\textstyle{z(X_{S^{\prime}}/S^{\prime},0)[1/p].}

Proof.

Let
g\colon\mathrm{Spec}(L)\rightarrow\mathrm{Spec}(K)
be an extension of fields, and
Y\rightarrow\mathrm{Spec}(K)
be a morphism of finite type.
Then the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}^{\mathrm{des}}_{0}(Y_{\overline{K}},K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{Y}}
\scriptstyle{g^{*}}
\textstyle{Z_{0}(Y)[1/p]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\otimes_{K}L}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}^{\mathrm{des}}_{0}(Y_{\overline{L}},L)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{d_{Y_{L}}}
\textstyle{Z_{0}(Y_{L})[1/p].}

Indeed, if
K
is algebraically closed, the commutativity follows since the residue field of any closed point of
Y
is equal to
K
.
In the general case, since the right vertical map is injective,
it suffices to show the case where
L
is an algebraic closure of
K
.
In this case, the verification is easy.

Let
R
be a discrete valuation ring with field of fractions
K
, and let
f\colon\mathrm{Spec}(R)\rightarrow S
be a dominant morphism.
Assume we are given a morphism
\mathrm{Spec}(\kappa)\rightarrow\mathrm{Spec}(R)
over the closed point, where
\kappa
is a field.
Let
h^{\prime}\colon X_{R}\rightarrow\mathrm{Spec}(R)
be the base change of
h
.
Let
\eta\rightsquigarrow s
be the specialization on
S
defined by
f
.
For a closed subscheme
Z\subset X_{K}
, recall from
[
SV
, 3.1.2]
that
\phi_{h^{\prime}}(Z)
is the closed subscheme of
X_{R}
such that

\phi_{h^{\prime}}(Z)\rightarrow\mathrm{Spec}(R)
is flat and
\phi_{h^{\prime}}(Z)\times_{\mathrm{Spec}(R)}\mathrm{Spec}(K)
is isomorphic to
Z
.
For
F=\{F_{\eta}\}_{\eta\in S^{0}}
in
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{0}(X/S)
, it suffices to show that

(
\star
)

\phi_{h^{\prime}}\bigl(d_{h_{\eta}}(F_{\eta})\otimes_{k(\eta)}K\bigr)\times_{\mathrm{Spec}(R)}\mathrm{Spec}(\kappa)=d_{h_{s}}(\Psi_{s\leftarrow\eta}(F))\otimes_{k(s)}\kappa.

Indeed, since
F
is a flat étale system, the right hand side only depends on the rational point
\mathrm{Spec}(\kappa)\rightarrow S
not on
f
.
Thus, by definition
[
SV
, 3.1.3]
, we have
d_{h}(F)\in\mathrm{Cycl}(X/S,0)[1/p]
.
To conclude, we note that
\mathrm{Cycl}(X/S,0)[1/p]=z(X/S,0)[1/p]
.
Indeed, the proof of
[
SV
, 3.3.14]
in fact shows that the abelian presheaf
\mathrm{Cycl}(X/S,r)/z(X/S,r)
is
p
-torsion.
It remains to show (

⋆

‣
4.9
).

In view of the commutative diagram above, we may assume that
\kappa=k(s)
,

\mathrm{Spec}(R)
is a strictly henselian trait, and
S=\mathrm{Spec}(R)
.
For any scheme
Y
of finite type over an algebraically closed field, we have
Z_{0}(Y)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}_{0}(Y)
,
where the map sends
\sum n_{i}[x_{i}]
to
\sum n_{i}[\Lambda_{x_{i}}]
.
Let
F\in\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}^{\mathrm{des}}_{0}(X_{\overline{\eta}},\eta)
.
By the isomorphism, we may write
F=\sum_{x_{i}\in|X_{\overline{\eta}}|}n_{i}[\Lambda_{x_{i}}]
where
n_{i}
are non-zero integers.
Let
W=\bigcup p(x_{i})
, where
p\colon X_{\overline{\eta}}\rightarrow X_{\eta}
.
Since
F
is descendable, it is the pullback of
\sum_{w\in|W|}n_{w}[\Lambda_{w}]
, where
n_{w}=n_{i}
for any
x_{i}
lying over
w
.
By definition, we have
\Psi_{s\leftarrow\eta}(F)=\sum n_{w}[\Psi_{s\leftarrow\eta}(\Lambda_{w})]
, and it suffices to show the claim for
F=p^{*}\Lambda_{w}
.
Let
W:=\{w\}^{\mathrm{norm}}\rightarrow X
, the normalization of the closure of
w
in
X
.
By the compatibility of proper pushforward and the nearby cycle functor, we may assume that
X=W
.
Namely,
h
is assumed to be finite and
X
is the spectrum of a discrete valuation ring.
Let
s_{W}
be the closed point of
W
, and
\eta_{W}=w
be the generic point.
In this case, we have

\displaystyle\Psi_{s\leftarrow\eta}(\Lambda_{\eta_{W}})=\Psi_{s\leftarrow\eta}(h_{*}\Lambda_{\eta_{W}})=[k(\eta_{W}):k(\eta)]_{\mathrm{sep}}\cdot[\Lambda_{s_{W}}],

\displaystyle\phi_{h}([\eta_{W}])\times_{S}s=[W\times_{S}s]=[k(\eta_{W}):k(\eta)]\cdot[s_{W}],

where
[,]_{\mathrm{sep}}
denotes the separable degree.
Thus, the claim follows.
∎

4.10

Let us recall the Artin-Schreier
\Lambda
-module.
Let
Y:=\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[T])\rightarrow\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbb{F}_{p}[t])
defined by sending
t
to
T^{p}-T
.
This is a Galois covering with Galois group canonically isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}
.
Let
\psi\colon\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\Lambda^{\times}
be a character.
Then, we get the representation

\pi_{1}(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}})\rightarrow\mathrm{Gal}(Y/\mathbb{A}^{1})\cong\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\psi}\Lambda^{\times}.

The associated smooth sheaf of rank
1
is denoted by
\mathcal{L}_{\psi}
.
Let
X
be a scheme over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, and assume we are given a morphism
f\colon X\rightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}_{\mathbb{F}_{p}}
.
Then the pullback
f^{*}\mathcal{L}_{\psi}
is denoted by
\mathcal{L}_{\psi}(f)
.

Remark
. —

For
d>0
, we do not have the homomorphism
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{d}(Y/T)\rightarrow z(Y/T,d)
.
For example, consider
T=\mathbb{A}_{t}^{1}
,
Y=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{T,x}
, and
F=[\mathcal{L}_{\psi}(x/t)]
.
Then the generic rank is
1
but the rank of
\Psi_{0\leftarrow\eta}(F)
is
0
.
In a similar vein,
\mathrm{Supp}(F)
is not necessarily in
z(Y/T,d)
when
d>0
.
For this, consider
T=\mathrm{Spec}(k[t,s]/(ts))
,
Y=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{T,x}
.
If we put
F=[\mathcal{L}_{\psi}(x/t)]
, this becomes a flat étale system because
\Psi_{0\leftarrow\eta_{t}}(F)=0
,
where
\eta_{t}
is the generic point of
T
corresponding to the irreducible component
\mathrm{Spec}(k[t])
.

5
Construction of localization system

In this section, we construct a localization system, and construct our characteristic cycle.
In the course of construction, we use one result whose proof will be postponed to Part II of this paper.
We keep the notations from §
4
.
Throughout this section, we fix an excellent separated
k
-scheme
S
of finite Krull dimension.
We also fix a non-trivial character
\psi\colon\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\Lambda^{\times}
,
and the locally constant sheaf
\mathcal{L}_{\psi}(f)
introduced in
4.10
is denoted simply by
\mathcal{L}(f)
.

5.1

Assume we are given a morphism
X\rightarrow S
of finite type.
We fix this morphism, and we will construct a localization system associated to it.
We fix a finite open covering
\{X_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq n}
of
X
such that
X_{i}\rightarrow S
is affine.
Since
S
is assumed to be separated, such covering always exists.

Construction of
\mu
:
Let
\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}/S}
be the presheaf on
\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
associating
\Gamma(X_{i}\times_{S}T,\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}\times_{S}T})
to
T
.
This presheaf is obviously pointed by
0
, and consider it as a pointed set.
We put
\mu_{X/S}:=\bigvee_{i}\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}/S}
.
Here,
\bigvee
is the coproduct in the category of pointed sets.
In other words,
\mu_{X/S}(T)=\coprod_{i}\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}/S}(T)/\sim
, where
0_{i}\sim 0_{i^{\prime}}
(
0_{i}\in\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}/S}(T)
) for any
1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq n
.

Let us construct a contraction morphism.
For
T
, take
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}/S}(T)
.
Let
s
be the coordinate of
\mathbb{A}^{1}
.
We have
s\cdot f\in\Gamma(T\times\mathbb{A}^{1},\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}/S})
, which defines a homomorphism
\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}/S}\rightarrow p_{*}p^{*}\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}/S}
.
The construction is functorial, the homomorphism preserves the base points, and the composition with restriction to
1\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
is the identity.
Moreover, the composition with restriction to
0\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
is the map sending
f\in\mu
to
0\in\mu
.
Thus, we have a morphism of presheaves
c_{\mu}\colon\mu_{X/S}\rightarrow p_{*}p^{*}\mu_{X/S}
, defining a contraction morphism.

5.2

To define a pre-localization system, we need to define a presheaf
\mathscr{S}
and a morphism
\mathcal{E}
.
The presheaf
\mathscr{S}
is easy to define:

Construction of
\mathscr{S}
:
We put
\mathscr{S}_{X/S}:=\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X/S}
(cf. Definition
4.8
).
This presheaf is equipped with the filtration
\{\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{d,X/S}\}_{d\geq 0}
, but we define a finer filtration on
\mathscr{S}
.
For
d>0
,
n\geq i\geq 0
, we put
\mathscr{S}_{d,i}
to be the following submodule:

\mathrm{Ker}\bigl(\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{d,X/S}\rightarrow\mathrm{gr}_{d}\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{U_{i}/S}\bigr),

where
U_{i}:=\bigcup_{j>i}X_{j}
.
In other words,
F=\{F_{\eta}\}_{\eta\in T^{0}}\in\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{d,X/S}(T)
belongs to

\mathscr{S}_{d,i}(T)
if
F_{\eta}|_{(U_{i})_{\overline{\eta}}}\in\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}_{d-1}((U_{i})_{\overline{\eta}})
for each
\eta\in T^{0}
.
We have
\mathscr{S}_{d,n}=\mathscr{S}_{d}
and
\mathscr{S}_{d,0}=\mathscr{S}_{d-1}
.
The filtration can be depicted as

\mathscr{S}_{0}=\mathscr{S}_{1,0}\subset\mathscr{S}_{1,1}\subset\dots\subset\mathscr{S}_{1,n}=\mathscr{S}_{1}=\mathscr{S}_{2,0}\subset\mathscr{S}_{2,1}\subset\dots.

Even though the filtration appears to be double filtered, we can make it a filtration indexed by
\mathbb{N}
by relabeling suitably.
It is exhaustive since if we take
d
to be greater than the relative dimension of
X
over
S
,
namely
\max\{\dim(X_{s});s\in S\}
, then
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{d,X/S}=\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{X/S}
.

5.3

To define the homomorphism
\mathcal{E}
, we will be using the following lemma:

Lemma
. —

Let
f\colon V\rightarrow T\times\Box
be a modification of
S
-schemes of finite type.
Then, there exists an open dense subscheme
U\subset T_{\mathrm{red}}
such that
f_{U}\colon V\times_{T}U\rightarrow U\times\Box
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Shrinking
T
and considering irreducible component-wise, we may assume that
T
is integral.
Since
S
is excellent and of finite dimension, the set of normal points of
T
is open, thus shrinking
T
further,
we may assume that
T
is normal of dimension
d<\infty
.
Let
W\subset T\times\Box
be the set of points
x\in T\times\Box
such that
\dim(f^{-1}(x))\geq 1
.
Then
W
is closed by [EGA IV, 13.1.3].
By Zariski main theorem [EGA III, 4.4.9],
f
is isomorphic outside of
W
.
If
\dim(W)\geq d
, then by [EGA IV, 5.6.8],
\dim(f^{-1}(W))\geq d+1
, and contradict with the assumption that
V
is irreducible.
Thus,
W
is of dimension
\leq d-1
.
By [EGA IV, 5.6.8],
\overline{p(W)}
in
T
, where
p\colon T\times\Box\rightarrow T
is the projection, is of dimension
\leq d-1
as well.
Thus, by [EGA IV, 5.1.8],
U:=T\setminus\overline{p(W)}
is an open dense subscheme which satisfies the condition.
∎

5.4

Let
T
be an
S
-scheme, and take
F\in\mathscr{S}_{d,i}(T)
for
i\geq 1
and
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}/S}(T)
.
Let
\mathbf{V}:=(V\rightarrow T^{\prime}\times\Box)
be an object in
\mathrm{Mdf}
over
\mathbf{T}\times\Box:=(T\times\Box\rightarrow T\times\Box)
,
and denote by
t
the coordinate of
\Box
.
Let
\pi\colon V\rightarrow T
and
j_{\overline{\eta}}\colon(X_{i})_{\overline{\eta}}\hookrightarrow X_{\overline{\eta}}
for each
\eta\in V^{0}
.
Since
(j_{\overline{\eta}})_{!}
is an exact functor, it induces a homomorphism between the Grothendieck groups, and we may consider

j_{\overline{\eta}!}\,j_{\overline{\eta}}^{*}(\pi^{*}F\otimes\mathcal{L}(ft))_{\eta}\in\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{\overline{\eta}})
where
\eta\in V^{0}
.
In view of Example
4.6
, this element is descendable.
We put

(F_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes\mathcal{L}(ft))_{\mathrm{gen}}:=\Bigl\{j_{\overline{\eta}!}j_{\overline{\eta}}^{*}(\pi^{*}F\otimes\mathcal{L}(ft))_{\eta}\Bigr\}\in\prod_{\eta\in V_{\mathrm{fl}}^{0}}\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}^{\mathrm{des}}(X_{\overline{\eta}},\eta).

Note that this construction only depends on the composition
V\rightarrow T^{\prime}\times\Box\rightarrow T\times\Box
, and not on
V\rightarrow T^{\prime}\times\Box
.
Of course, this element is not a flat étale system in general.
We say that
\mathbf{V}
is
adapted to
(F,f)
if there exists
C=\{C_{\eta}\}_{\eta\in V^{0}_{\mathrm{fl}}}\in\mathscr{S}_{d,i}(V_{\mathrm{fl}})
such that

C_{\eta}|_{(X_{i})_{\overline{\eta}}}=(F_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes\mathcal{L}(ft))_{\eta}\,|_{(X_{i})_{\overline{\eta}}}.

The flat étale system
C
is determined uniquely up to differences in
\mathscr{S}_{d,i-1}(V_{\mathrm{fl}})
.
Indeed, assume we are given another
C^{\prime}
satisfying the condition.
Then we have
\bigl(C_{\eta}-C^{\prime}_{\eta}\bigr)|_{(X_{i})_{\overline{\eta}}}=0
by the condition.
On the other hand, since
C^{(\prime)}\in\mathscr{S}_{d,i}
,
the restriction
C^{(\prime)}_{\eta}|_{(U_{i})_{\overline{\eta}}}
belongs to
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}_{d-1}((U_{i})_{\overline{\eta}})
.
Combining these, the restriction of
C_{\eta}-C^{\prime}_{\eta}
to
U_{i-1}:=\bigcup_{j\geq i}X_{j}=U_{i}\cup X_{i}
belongs to

\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}_{d-1}((U_{i-1})_{\overline{\eta}})
, and
C-C^{\prime}
belongs to
\mathscr{S}_{d,i-1}
as required.
We denote the flat étale system
C
in
\mathrm{gr}_{d,i}\mathscr{S}(V_{\mathrm{fl}})

by
F[\mathbf{V}]
, or more precisely,
(F;f)[\mathbf{V}]
.
Let us denote this sieve generated by objects
\mathbf{V}
adapted to
(F;f)
is denoted by
\mathrm{R}(F;f)
.

Lemma
. —

1.

The sieve
\mathrm{R}(F;f)
is a covering sieve of
\mathbf{T}\times\Box
.

2.

Let
\phi\colon\mathbf{V}^{\prime}\rightarrow\mathbf{V}
be a morphism in
\mathrm{Mdf}
.
Assume that
\mathbf{V}
is adapted to
(F;f)
.
Then
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}
is adapted as well, and we have
\phi_{\mathrm{fl}}^{*}F[\mathbf{V}]=F[\mathbf{V}^{\prime}]
in
\mathrm{gr}_{d,i}\mathscr{S}(V^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fl}})
,
where
\phi_{\mathrm{fl}}\colon V^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fl}}\rightarrow V_{\mathrm{fl}}
is the morphism induced by
\phi
.

Proof.

Let us show the first claim.
We may assume that
T
is reduced.
It suffices to find a cdp-covering
\{V_{i}\}\rightarrow V
such that
V_{i}\rightarrow T\times\Box
is adapted to
(F;f)
.
First, there exists a modification
V\rightarrow T\times\Box
over which
(F_{\mathbf{T}\times\Box}\otimes\mathcal{L}(ft))_{\mathrm{gen}}
,
becomes a flat étale system by Lemma
4.8
.
By definition, this
V
is adapted to
(F;f)
.
By Lemma
Lemma
, there exists an open dense subscheme
U_{0}\subset T
such that
V\times_{T}U_{0}\rightarrow U_{0}\times\Box
is an isomorphism.
Put
T_{1}:=T\setminus U_{0}
, and we can take a modification
V_{1}
of
T_{1}\times\Box
over which

(F_{\mathbf{T}_{1}\times\Box}\otimes\mathcal{L}(ft))_{\mathrm{gen}}
becomes a flat étale system.
Using the lemma, we can take a closed subscheme over which
V_{1}\rightarrow T_{1}\times\Box
is an isomorphism.
We iteratively construct
T_{i}
,
V_{i}
, and this process terminates in finitely many steps since
T
is noetherian.
Then
\{V_{i}\rightarrow T\times\Box\}
is a desired covering.

Let us show the second claim.
We take a lifting of
F[\mathbf{V}]
in
\mathscr{S}_{d,i}
, and abusively denote by
F[\mathbf{V}]
.
By construction of
F[\mathbf{V}^{\prime}]
, it suffices to show that

(F_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{L}(ft))_{\eta^{\prime}}|_{(X_{i})_{\overline{\eta}^{\prime}}}=\phi_{\mathrm{fl}}^{*}(F[\mathbf{V}]|_{(X_{i})_{\overline{\eta}^{\prime}}})

in
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}((X_{i})_{\overline{\eta}^{\prime}})
for each
\eta^{\prime}\in V^{\prime 0}_{\mathrm{fl}}
.
Thus, we may assume that
X=X_{i}
.
In this situation,
f
is a regular function.
Take a generic point
\eta^{\prime}
of
V^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fl}}
.
By definition of
V^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fl}}
, the image of
\eta^{\prime}
in
\Box
is the generic point of
\Box
.
Thus, we can find a specialization
\eta\rightsquigarrow\phi(\eta^{\prime})
from a generic point
\eta
of
V_{\mathrm{fl}}
.
The situation can be depicted as below.

\textstyle{V^{\prime}_{\mathrm{fl}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi_{\mathrm{fl}}}
\textstyle{\eta^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{V_{\mathrm{fl}}}
\textstyle{\eta\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\phi(\eta^{\prime})}

By the choice,
\phi(\eta^{\prime})
sits over the generic point of
\Box
, and
f|_{V}t
is regular around
\phi(\eta^{\prime})
.
Thus,
\mathcal{L}(f|_{V}t)
is smooth around
\phi(\eta^{\prime})
, which implies

\Psi_{\phi(\eta^{\prime})\leftarrow\eta}\bigl(F_{V}\otimes\mathcal{L}(f|_{V}t)\bigr)=F_{\phi(\eta^{\prime})}\otimes\mathcal{L}(f|_{V}t).

By definition, this means that

(F_{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{L}(ft))_{\eta^{\prime}}|_{X_{\overline{\eta}^{\prime}}}=\phi_{\mathrm{fl}}^{*}(F[\mathbf{V}]|_{X_{\overline{\eta}^{\prime}}})
.
∎

Now, we are ready to construct
\mathcal{E}
.

Construction of
\mathcal{E}
:
Assume we are given an element
F\in\mathscr{S}_{d,i}(T)
, and
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X_{j}/S}(T)
for some
j
.
If
j\neq i
, we simply put
\mathcal{E}_{X/S}(F;f)=F
.
The map is non-trivial only in the case
i=j
.
In this case, the above lemma implies that
\{F[\mathbf{V}]\}_{\mathbf{V}\in\mathrm{R}(F;f)}
determines an element of

\mathop{\underleftarrow{\mathrm{lim}}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{V}\in\mathrm{R}(F;f)}\Gamma(V_{\mathrm{fl}},\mathrm{gr}_{d,i}(\mathscr{S}))\cong\mathop{\underleftarrow{\mathrm{lim}}}\nolimits_{\mathbf{V}\in\mathrm{R}(F;f)}\Gamma(\mathbf{V},\mathrm{fl}^{*}\mathrm{gr}_{d,i}(\mathscr{S})).

Since
\mathrm{R}
is a covering sieve of
\mathbf{T}\times\Box
, this element determines an element of
\pi_{0}\partial\mathrm{gr}_{d,i}(\mathscr{S})(T)
.
The construction does not depend on the differences by
\mathscr{S}_{d,i-1}
, and it induces the morphism

\mathrm{gr}_{d,i}\mathscr{S}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}/S}(T)\rightarrow\pi_{0}\partial\mathrm{gr}_{d,i}\mathscr{S}(T)
.
This element is defined to be
\mathcal{E}(F;f)
.
These morphisms define a morphism of presheaves because the construction of

(F_{\mathbf{V}}\otimes\mathcal{L}(ft))_{\mathrm{gen}}
only depends on the morphism
V\rightarrow T
.
Note that
\mathcal{E}(F;0)=F
for
0\in\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}/S}(T)
, so the maps glue together to get

\mathscr{S}_{d,i}\otimes\mu\rightarrow\pi_{0}\partial\mathrm{gr}_{d,i}\mathscr{S}
.
Composing with
\pi_{0}\partial\mathrm{gr}_{d,i}\mathscr{S}\rightarrow\partial\mathrm{gr}_{d,i}\mathscr{S}
, we define the morphism
\mathcal{E}
.

Summing up, for a morphism
X\rightarrow S
of finite type,
and for a choice of finite open covering
\{X_{i}\}
of
X
such that
X_{i}\rightarrow S
is affine,
we have the pre-localization system
\mathrm{LSys}_{X/S}:=(\mathscr{S}_{X/S},\mu_{X/S},\mathcal{E}_{X/S})
over
S
.
This pre-localization system
does
depend on the choice of the open coverings,
but since merely the existence is important for us, we do not put this data in the notation.

5.5

Let us give a variant of the previous construction.
Take an
S
-scheme
T
,
F\in\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}(X_{T}/T)
,
\mathbf{V}
, and
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X/S}(T)
, as before.
We may consider
\{(\pi^{*}F\otimes\mathcal{L}(ft))_{\eta}\}_{\eta\in V^{0}_{\mathrm{fl}}}
as well.
This time, since
f
is defined everywhere, we have a homomorphism

\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{X/S}\colon\mathscr{S}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X/S}\rightarrow\partial\mathscr{S}.

Note that, by construction, the composition
\mathscr{S}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X/S}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}\partial\mathscr{S}\rightarrow\partial_{0}\mathscr{S}

is isomorphic to the canonical map
\mathscr{S}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X/S}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}
.
This morphism endows
\mathscr{S}
with a support structure, and will be used for microlocalization later.

5.6

To show that the pre-localization systems we defined is a localization system, let us analyze the condition that
\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{n}(F;\mathbf{f})=0

for
F\in\mathscr{S}_{d}(T)
for an integer
d>0
.
First, we introduce some terminologies.
Fix
T\in\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
.
For
T^{\prime}
over
T
, and
\mathbf{V}:=(V\rightarrow T^{\prime}\times\Box)\in\mathrm{Mdf}_{T^{\prime}}
, let
\mathbf{V}_{\infty}:=V_{\mathrm{fl}}\times_{\Box}\{\infty\}
.
This is a
T^{\prime}
-scheme.
An
\mathrm{Mdf}
-sequence over
T
(
of length
n\geq 0
) is a set
\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\in\mathrm{Mdf}_{\mathbf{V}_{i-1,\infty}}\}_{i=1,\dots,n}

such that
\mathbf{V}_{0}:=(T\times\Box\rightarrow T\times\Box)
.
The sequence is said to be
trivial
if
\mathbf{V}_{i}=(T\times\Box\rightarrow T\times\Box)
for any
i
.
A morphism of
\mathrm{Mdf}
-sequences
\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}\rightarrow\{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{i}\}
is a collection of morphisms
V_{i}\rightarrow V^{\prime}_{i}

compatible with
\mathbf{V}_{i-1,\infty}\rightarrow\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{i-1,\infty}
.
Similarly to the definition of
\mathcal{E}
, we define as follows:

Definition
. —

Assume that
X_{T}
and
T
are affine, and let
F\in\mathscr{S}_{d}(T)
,
\mathbf{f}:=(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\in\mathcal{O}_{X_{T}}(T)^{\infty}
.

1.

We say that an
\mathrm{Mdf}
-sequence
\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}
is
adapted to
(F;\mathbf{f})
if the following condition holds:
Put
F[\emptyset]_{\infty}:=F
, and assume
F[\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}_{i<k}]_{\infty}\in\mathscr{S}(\mathbf{V}_{n,\infty})

is defined.
Then,
F[\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}_{i<k}]_{\infty}\otimes\mathcal{L}(f_{k}t)
is a flat étale system on
\mathbf{V}_{k}
.
We let
F[\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}_{i\leq k}]_{\infty}
be the restriction to
\mathbf{V}_{k,\infty}
of this flat étale system.

2.

An
\mathrm{Mdf}
-sequence
\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}
is said to be a
principal sequence

if each morphism
\mathbf{V}_{i}\colon V_{i}\rightarrow\mathbf{V}_{i-1,\infty}\times\Box
is surjective.

We put
\mathcal{E}_{x}:=r_{x}\circ\mathcal{E}
, where
r_{x}
is the one in
1.3
.
Using this notion, we may detect the kernel of
\mathcal{E}^{n}_{\infty}
as follows:

Lemma
. —

Let
F\in\mathscr{S}_{d}(T)
, and
\mathbf{f}:=(f_{1},\dots,f_{n})\in\mathcal{O}_{X_{T}}(T)^{\infty}
.

1.

Let
\phi=\{\phi_{i}\}\colon\{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{i}\}\rightarrow\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}
be a morphism of
\mathrm{Mdf}
-sequences of length
n
adapted to
(F;\mathbf{f})
.
Then
F[\{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{i}\}]_{\infty}=\phi^{*}_{n}F[\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}]_{\infty}
.

2.

Let
\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}
be a principal
\mathrm{Mdf}
-sequence of length
n
, and
\{\mathbf{W}_{i}\}
be any
\mathrm{Mdf}
-sequence of length
n
.
Then, we can find a diagram
\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}\leftarrow\{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{i}\}\rightarrow\{\mathbf{W}_{i}\}
such that

\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{i,\infty}\rightarrow\mathbf{W}_{i,\infty}
is surjective for any
i\leq n
.

3.

A principal
\mathrm{Mdf}
-sequence adapted to
(F;\mathbf{f})
exists.

4.

If there exists a principal sequence
\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}
adapted to
(F;\mathbf{f})

such that
F[\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}]_{\infty}
is in
\mathscr{S}_{d-1}
, then
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}^{n}(F;\mathbf{f})=0
.

Proof.

The first claim follows by the functoriality of flat étale systems, and can be checked as in Lemma
5.4
.
2
.
Let us show the second claim. We use the induction on the length. For
n=0
, the claim is obvious.
Assume that the claim is true for
n<l
. We show the claim for
n=l
.
We have already constructed the following diagram of solid arrows:

\textstyle{\mathbf{V}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{W}_{n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}_{n-1,\infty}\times\Box}
\textstyle{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{n-1,\infty}\times\Box\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{W}_{n-1,\infty}\times\Box.}

We can find
Z
which is of finite type rendering the diagram commutative with the vertical dotted arrow proper.
By putting
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{n}:=(Z\rightarrow\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{n-1,\infty}\times\Box)
, the sequence
\{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{n}\}
has the desired properties.

Let us show the third claim.
Let
\mathbf{V}_{1}:=(V_{1}\rightarrow T\times\Box)
be a modification over which
F\otimes\mathcal{L}(f_{1}t)
becomes a flat étale system.
Then put
F_{1}:=(F\otimes\mathcal{L}(f_{1}t))|_{\mathbf{V}_{1,\infty}}
.
Then take a modification
\mathbf{V}_{2}:=(V_{2}\rightarrow\mathbf{V}_{1,\infty}\times\Box)
so that
F_{1}\otimes\mathcal{L}(f_{2}t)
becomes a flat étale system.
Put
F_{2}:=(F_{1}\otimes\mathcal{L}(f_{2}t))|_{\mathbf{V}_{2,\infty}}
, and repeat this process till we construct
\mathbf{V}_{n}
,
F_{n}
.
Then
\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}
is a principal
\mathrm{Mdf}
-sequence adapted to
(F;\mathbf{f})
.

Let us show the last claim.
Let
\mathbb{W}:=\{\mathbf{W}_{i}\}_{i\leq n}
be an
\mathrm{Mdf}
-sequence of
T
adapted to
(F;\mathbf{f})
.
Let
\mathbb{W}_{\leq k}:=\{\mathbf{W}_{i}\}_{i\leq k}
.
Fix
\{\mathbf{W}_{i}\}_{i<n}
and vary
\mathbf{W}_{n}
.
Then the family
\{F[\mathbb{W}]_{\infty}\}_{\mathbf{W}_{n}}

determines an element
F\left<\mathbb{W}_{<n}\right>_{\infty}
of
\partial_{\infty}\mathscr{S}(\mathbf{W}_{n-1,\infty})
.
By
1
, this element is functorial with respect to varying
\mathbf{W}_{n-1}
fixing
\mathbb{W}_{\leq n-2}
.
Thus, the family
\{F\left<\mathbb{W}_{\leq n-1}\right>_{\infty}\}_{\mathbf{W}_{n-1}}
determines an element of
\partial^{2}_{\infty}\mathscr{S}(\mathbf{W}_{n-2,\infty})
.
Repeating this,
\{F[\mathbb{W}]_{\infty}\}_{\mathbb{W}}
determines an element of
\partial^{n}_{\infty}\mathscr{S}(T)
,
which is nothing but
\mathcal{E}^{n}_{\infty}(F;\mathbf{f})
.
If
F[\mathbb{W}]_{\infty}
belongs to
\mathscr{S}_{d-1}(\mathbf{W}_{n,\infty})
for any
\mathbb{W}
, then
\mathcal{E}^{n}_{\infty}(F;\mathbf{f})=0
.
Thus, in order to show the claim, it suffices to check that
F[\mathbb{W}]_{\infty}\in\mathscr{S}_{d-1}
for any
\mathbb{W}
.
By
2
, we can find a diagram
\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}\leftarrow\{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{i}\}\rightarrow\{\mathbf{W}_{i}\}
such that

\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{n}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{W}_{n}
.
By
1
,
F[\{\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{i}\}]
belongs to
\mathscr{S}_{d-1}(\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{n,\infty})
.
The surjectivity of
\mathbf{V}^{\prime}_{n}\twoheadrightarrow\mathbf{W}_{n}
and
1
implies
that
F[\mathbb{W}]_{\infty}\in\mathscr{S}_{d-1}(\mathbf{W}_{n,\infty})
.
∎

5.7

The main result of the second part is the following existence result.
Since the proof is completely of different flavor and independent, we postpone the proof to Part II.

Theorem

(Main result of [Part II])
. —

Assume that
X\rightarrow S
is a morphism of finite type between noetherian affine schemes over
k

(
not necessarily of finite type
)
.
For any
F\in\mathscr{S}_{d}(S)
,
m\geq 0
, and
\mathbf{f}\in\mathcal{O}_{X\times\Delta^{m}}(X\times\Delta^{m})^{\times l}
,
we can find
\mathbf{g}\in\mathcal{O}_{X}(X)^{\times l^{\prime}}
such that for a principal
\mathrm{Mdf}
-sequence
\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}
adapted to
(F;\mathbf{f}\vee\mathbf{g})
,

F[\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}]_{\infty}
is in
\mathscr{S}_{d-1}
.

Using this theorem, we use the following vanishing result:

Corollary
. —

We keep the notation.
For any
F\in\mathscr{S}_{d}(S)
and
\mathbf{f}\in\mathcal{O}_{X\times\Delta^{m}}(X\times\Delta^{m})^{\times l}
,
we can find
\mathbf{g}\in\mathcal{O}_{X_{T}}(X_{T})^{\times l^{\prime}}
such that
\bm{\mathcal{E}}^{n}(F;\mathbf{f}\vee\mathbf{g})=0
for
n
sufficiently large.

Proof.

We have
\mathcal{E}_{0}(G,f)-I(F)=0
for any
f\in\mathcal{O}_{X\times\Delta^{m}}(X\times\Delta^{m})
and
G\in\mathscr{S}_{d}(T)
,
since the function is defined entirely on
X_{T}\times\Delta^{m}
.
Thus, it suffices find
\mathbf{g}
so that
\mathcal{E}_{\infty}^{n}(F;\mathbf{f}\vee\mathbf{g})=0
.
By the theorem, we can find
\mathbf{g}
so that for a principal
\mathrm{Mdf}
-sequence
\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}
adapted to
(F;\mathbf{f}\vee\mathbf{g})
,

F[\{\mathbf{V}_{i}\}]_{\infty}
belongs to
\mathscr{S}_{d-1}
.
By Lemma
Lemma
.
4
, the corollary follows.
∎

5.8 Theorem
. —

The pre-localization system
(\mathscr{S}_{X/S},\mu_{X/S},\mathcal{E}_{X/S})
is in fact a localization system.

Proof.

We must check the condition of Definition
Definition
for each piece of the filtration.
Assume we are given
(F;\mathbf{f})\in(\mathscr{S}_{d,i}\otimes\mu^{\infty})(T\times\Delta^{m})
.
It suffices to find a finite sequence
\mathbf{g}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X_{i}/S}(T)\subset\mu_{X/S}(T)
Zariski locally with respect to each
t\in T

so that
(F;\mathbf{f}\vee\mathbf{g})
is in
\mathrm{Adm}
.
Shrinking
T
around
t
, we may assume that
T
is affine.
Since we took
X_{i}\rightarrow S
to be affine,
(X_{i})_{T}
is affine as well.
Then the condition is exactly the content of Corollary
5.7
.
∎

Construction of characteristic cycles

5.9

Now, let us construct the characteristic cycle.
First let us explain the construction of non-microlocalized characteristic cycles fixing a morphism
X\rightarrow S
.
Logically, this paragraph is unnecessary, but this should make it easier to understand the main idea.

Let
X\rightarrow S
be a morphism, and consider the localization system
\mathrm{LSys}_{X/S}
we have just constructed.
Assume we are given a six functor formalism as in
2.10
,
and recall from Definition
2.20
that we have a reversible coefficient
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X/S}
.
We also assume that
\mathrm{char}(k)
is invertible in
R
.
Let
\underline{z}_{X/S}^{\mathrm{SV}}(n)\colon\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}
be the functor sending
T
to the abelian group
z(X_{T}/T,n)\otimes R
.
We have the morphism of presheaves:

\mathscr{S}_{X/S,0}\rightarrow\underline{z}^{\mathrm{SV}}_{X/S}(0)\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X/S},

where the first homomorphism is constructed in
4.9
, and the second by the trace map
C.3
.
By Theorem
5.8
,
\mathscr{S}_{X/S}
admits a structure of localization system whose
0
-th piece is
\mathscr{S}_{X/S,0}
.
Thus, by Theorem
1.11
, the above homomorphism extends (essentially uniquely) to a homomorphism

\mathrm{CC}_{X}\colon\mathscr{S}_{X/S}\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X/S}.

By taking the global section and taking
\pi_{0}
, we have the homomorphism of
R
-modules

\mathrm{CC}\colon\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}(X/S)\rightarrow\pi_{0}\Gamma(S,\mathscr{S}_{X/S})\rightarrow\pi_{0}\Gamma(S,\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X/S})\simeq\pi_{0}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(X/S,R_{S}).

This homomorphism is called the (
non-microlocalized
)
characteristic cycle homomorphism
.
Note that, in the case
S=\mathrm{Spec}(k)
where
k
is perfect and if we take the six functor formalism to be the
one associated with the
\mathbb{Z}[1/p]
-motivic cohomology theory (cf. Example
C.1
),

\pi_{0}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(X/S,\mathbb{Z}[1/p])\cong\mathrm{CH}_{0}(X)[1/p]
and
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}(X/S)\cong\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X)
.
We wish to microlocalize the construction by means of §
3
.
For this, we need to make this construction far more functorial as follows.

5.10

We have an abelian presheaf
z_{X/S}\colon(X/S)_{\mathrm{zar}}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}

sending
U\rightarrow T
to
z(U/T,0)\otimes R
.
In
C.4
, we defined the
\infty
-presheaf

{}^{\mathrm{open}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X/S}\colon(X/S)_{\mathrm{zar}}^{\mathrm{op}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Mod}_{R}

sending
U\rightarrow T
to
\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(U/T,R_{T})
.
In view of the exact sequence
[
SV
, 4.3.1]
and the construction of
{}^{\mathrm{open}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X/S}
as well as Lemma
C.7
.
2
,
the trace map induces the morphism of functors
z_{X/S}\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{open}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X/S}
.

On the other hand, we have the
\infty
-presheaf
\mathscr{S}_{X/S}
equipped with filtration on
(X/S)_{\mathrm{zar}}
.
By Lemma
Lemma
, we have the morphism
\mathscr{S}_{0}\rightarrow z_{X/S}
of abelian sheaves.
Combining together, we have the diagram
\mathscr{S}\leftarrow\mathscr{S}_{0}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{CC}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}
in
\mathrm{Fun}((X/S)^{\mathrm{op}}_{\mathrm{zar}},\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Now, the functor
(X/S)_{\mathrm{zar}}\rightarrow\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}
sending
U\rightarrow T
to
T\rightarrow S
is a Cartesian fibration.
The above diagram is equivalent to a diagram in
\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}^{\mathrm{op}}}((X/S)^{\mathrm{op}}_{\mathrm{zar}},\pi^{*}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}})
.
Thus, we get a diagram
\underline{\mathscr{S}}\leftarrow\underline{\mathscr{S}}_{0}\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}
in

\mathrm{Fun}_{\mathrm{Sch}_{/S}^{\mathrm{op}}}((X/S)^{\mathrm{op}}_{\mathrm{zar}},\mathcal{S}_{\pi^{*}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}})
.
For each object
U/T\in(X/S)_{\mathrm{zar}}
, the pair
(\underline{\mathscr{S}}_{0}(U/T),\underline{\mathscr{S}}(U/T))
is a localization pair in the sense of
1.11

by Theorem
5.8
.
On the other hand,
\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}(U/T)
is reversible by Definition
2.20
.
Thus, invoking Corollary
1.11
, the morphism
\underline{\mathscr{S}}_{0}\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}
extends essentially uniquely to a morphism

\underline{\mathscr{S}}\rightarrow\underline{\mathrm{H}}^{\mathrm{BM}}
.
By applying
\Gamma_{\pi^{*}\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}}}
(cf.
2.1
), we get a map
\mathrm{CC}_{X/S}\colon\mathscr{S}_{X/S}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}
in

\mathrm{Fun}((X/S)^{\mathrm{op}}_{\mathrm{zar}},\mathrm{Mod}_{\mathbb{Z}})
.
This is the functorial version of non-microlocalized characteristic cycle map.

Now, let us consider the functoriality.
Assume we are given a proper morphism
h\colon X\rightarrow Y
over
S
.
Since the functor
(Y/S)_{\mathrm{zar}}\rightarrow(X/S)_{\mathrm{zar}}
sending
(V/T)
to
(V\times_{Y}X/T)

is continuous and commutes with finite limits fiberwise, it induces the geometric morphism
\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}((X/S)_{\mathrm{zar}})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\mathrm{hv}((Y/S)_{\mathrm{zar}})
, also denoted by
h
.
Since the pushforward functor along proper morphism and the nearby cycle functor commute,
we have the following morphism (on the left) and homotopy commutative diagram (on the right):

(
\star
)

\textstyle{h_{*}\mathscr{S}_{X/S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{S}_{Y/S},}

\textstyle{h_{*}\mathscr{S}_{X/S,0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{CC}_{X/S}}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{h_{*}{}^{\mathrm{open}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X/S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{\mathscr{S}_{Y/S,0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{CC}_{Y/S}}
\textstyle{{}^{\mathrm{open}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{Y/S}.}

Now, we have the following diagram (which is possibly not commutative for the moment):

\textstyle{h_{*}\mathscr{S}_{X/S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{CC}_{X/S}}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\textstyle{h_{*}{}^{\mathrm{open}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X/S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}

In fact this diagram is homotopy commutative.
Indeed, by invoking Corollary
5.8
, the morphism

\mathrm{Map}(h_{*}\mathscr{S}_{X/S},{}^{\mathrm{open}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{Y/S})\rightarrow\mathrm{Map}(h_{*}\mathscr{S}_{X/S,0},{}^{\mathrm{open}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{Y/S})
is an equivalence.
Thus, we must show the homotopy commutativity of the diagram after restricting to
h_{*}\mathscr{S}_{X/S,0}
,
but this is nothing but the homotopy commutative diagram (

⋆

‣
5.10
).
It is worth noting that we did not make any smoothness assumption so far.
In this sense, our non-microlocalized characteristic cycle is a generalization of that of Saito that we will recall in the next section.

5.11

Finally, we will microlocalize our characteristic cycle.
Let
X\rightarrow S
be a scheme of finite type, and we have constructed the morphism
\mathscr{S}_{X/S}\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{open}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X/S}

of
\infty
-presheaves on
(X/S)_{\mathrm{zar}}
.
If
X
is
smooth
over
S
, we consider the base data
(X,T^{*}(X/S),\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathrm{d}_{X})
(cf. Example
3.1
),
where
\mathrm{d}_{X}\colon\mathcal{O}_{X}\rightarrow\Omega^{1}_{X/S}
is the usual differential.
We have constructed in
5.5
the homomorphism
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\colon\mathscr{S}_{X/S}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X/S}\rightarrow\partial\mathscr{S}_{X/S}

satisfying the condition of Definition
3.6
.
Thus it defines a support structure.
By the construction of
3.7
, we have the (
microlocalized
)
characteristic cycle map

\mathrm{CC}^{\mu}_{X/S}\colon(\mathscr{S}_{X/S})_{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{*}_{C}\bigl(T^{*}(X/S)/X,\omega_{X/S}\bigr).

The formation is compatible with base change of
S
by smooth morphism
S^{\prime}\rightarrow S
by
3.8
.

5.12

Now, let us establish the functoriality.
Assume we are given a proper morphism
h\colon X\rightarrow Y
of smooth
S
-schemes of finite type.
Consider the canonical morphisms
T^{*}(X/S)\xleftarrow{g}T^{*}(Y/S)\times_{Y}X\xrightarrow{h^{\prime}}T^{*}(Y/S)
.
We will construct the following homotopy commutative diagrams:

\textstyle{(\mathscr{S}_{X/S})_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

1

○
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{C}(T^{*}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathscr{S}_{X/S})_{g^{-1}(C)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{g^{-1}(C)}(T^{*}Y\times_{Y}X),}
\textstyle{(\mathscr{S}_{X/S})_{g^{-1}(C)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

2

○
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{g^{-1}(C)}(T^{*}Y\times_{Y}X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(h_{*}\mathscr{S}_{X/S})_{h^{\prime}g^{-1}(C)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{h^{\prime}g^{-1}(C)}(T^{*}Y),}

\textstyle{(h_{*}\mathscr{S}_{X/S})_{h^{\prime}g^{-1}(C)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

3

○
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{h^{\prime}g^{-1}(C)}(T^{*}Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(\mathscr{S}_{Y/S})_{h^{\prime}g^{-1}(C)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{h^{\prime}g^{-1}(C)}(T^{*}Y).}

These diagrams all follow from
3.8
.
The diagram

1

○
follows from the functoriality of base data for the morphism

(X,T^{*}(Y/S)\times_{Y}X,h^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{Y},\mathrm{d}_{Y})\rightarrow(X,T^{*}(X/S),\mathcal{O}_{X},\mathrm{d}_{X})
.
The diagram

2

○
follows from the functoriality of the pushforward via the morphism of schemes
h
.
The diagram

3

○
follows from the functoriality with respect to the homomorphism of presheaves
h_{*}\mathscr{S}_{X/S}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{Y/S}
.
Combining these diagrams, we have the following theorem.

5.13 Theorem
. —

Recall that
S
is an excellent scheme of finite Krull dimension,
and we are fixing a six functor formalism as in
2.10
such that the ring of coefficient
R
contains
\mathrm{char}(k)^{-1}
.
Let
h\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a proper morphism between smooth
S
-schemes of finite type, and let
C
be a conic in
T^{*}(X/S)
.
Consider the canonical morphisms
T^{*}(X/S)\xleftarrow{g}T^{*}(Y/S)\times_{Y}X\xrightarrow{h^{\prime}}T^{*}(Y/S)
, and let
h_{\circ}(C):=h^{\prime}(g^{-1}(C))
.
We have the following homotopy commutative diagram:

\textstyle{(\mathscr{S}_{X/S})_{C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{CC}^{\mu}_{X/S}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{C}(T^{*}(X/S),\omega_{X/S})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{h^{\prime}_{*}g^{*}}
\textstyle{(\mathscr{S}_{Y/S})_{h_{\circ}C}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{CC}^{\mu}_{Y/S}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{*}_{h_{\circ}C}(T^{*}(Y/S),\omega_{Y/S}).}

Remark
. —

For a separated scheme
X
of finite type over
S
and a closed embedding
i\colon X\hookrightarrow P
such that
P
is a smooth separated
S
-scheme,
we may consider the base data
(X,T^{*}(P/S)\times_{P}X,i^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{P},i^{-1}\mathrm{d}_{P})
.
We endow
\mathscr{S}_{X/S}
with the support structure

\mathscr{S}_{X/S}\otimes i^{-1}\mathcal{O}_{P}\rightarrow\mathscr{S}_{X/S}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X}\xrightarrow{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{X/S}}\partial\mathscr{S}_{X/S}
.
Then we have the characteristic cycle map
(\mathscr{S}_{X/S})_{C}\rightarrow\mathrm{H}^{*}_{C}(T^{*}(P/S)\times_{P}X,\omega_{X/S})
.
We also have a variant of the pushforward formula if we are given a morphism
P\rightarrow Y
between smooth
S
-schemes
such that the composition
X\rightarrow Y
is proper.
This is exactly the form conjectured by Saito in
[
S2
, Conjecture 1]
.
Since the verification is the same, we leave the details to the reader.

5.14
Summary

For the convenience of the reader, let us summarize what we have proven in specific cases of interest.
Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
, and
S
be a
k
-scheme of finite type.
We choose the six functor formalism associated to the
\mathbb{Z}[1/p]
-motivic cohomology theory (cf. Example
C.1
).
Then we may apply the theorem above.
If we take
S=\mathrm{Spec}(k)
, we have
\pi_{0}\mathrm{H}^{*}_{C}(T^{*}(X/S),\omega_{X/S})\cong\mathrm{CH}_{\dim(X)}(C)[1/p]
.
Thus, by taking
\pi_{0}
in the homotopy commutative diagram of Theorem
5.13
,
we get a similar equality as the one in Theorem of Introduction for our characteristic cycle.
At this moment, we have not proven the coincidence between our characteristic cycle and Saito’s.
This will be proven in the next section.

Remark
. —

1.

In the case where
S
is a
k
-scheme of finite type, we have another interesting consequence,
at least for non-microlocalized characteristic cycles.
Since the non-microlocalized characteristic cycle homomorphism

\mathrm{CC}_{X/S}\colon\mathscr{S}_{X/S}\rightarrow{}^{\mathrm{open}}\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}_{X/S}
is a homomorphism of
presheaves
,
it contains interesting functorial information by itself.
For example, assume
S
is a smooth integral curve with generic point
\eta
and
k
is separably closed.
Then we have a map
\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{\eta})\rightarrow\Gamma(X/S,\mathscr{S}_{X/S})

since any sheaf on
X
is already good over
S
.
Let
i\colon s\hookrightarrow S
be a closed immersion from a closed point
s
.
Then the functoriality of
\mathrm{CC}_{X/S}
implies that the following diagram commutes

\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{\eta})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{CC}_{X_{\eta}/\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\Psi_{s\leftarrow\overline{\eta}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(X/S)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{*}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{K}_{0}\mathrm{Cons}(X_{s})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{CC}_{X_{s}/s}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{H}^{\mathrm{BM}}(X_{s}/s).}

This can be regarded as a compatibility of the characteristic cycle homomorphism and the nearby cycle functor.
This can even be microlocalized, but for this, we need to modify the definition of microsupport in Definition
3.6
.
More precisely, in the definition, we considered only
T\rightarrow S
which is smooth,
but we need to consider morphisms of the form
T\rightarrow Z\hookrightarrow S

where the first morphism is smooth and the second one is an immersion.
The detail is left to the interested reader.

2.

The assumption that
S
is of finite type over a field
k
is made merely
because the six functor formalism for motivic cohomology satisfying the conditions of
2.10
has been constructed only under the assumption.
We did not try particularly hard to lift these assumptions.
In view of the previous remark, checking the assumptions of
2.10
in the case where
S
is a trait may be of interest.

3.

In the theorem above, we need to invert
\mathrm{char}(k)
.
We have two places where we need to make this assumption.
First is to construct the trace map in the motivic setting in
[
A2
]
.
If we assume the existence of the resolution of singularities, this assumption can be lifted.
More seriously, we need the inversion in the construction of the homomorphism
r
in
4.9
.
For this construction, the inversion is indispensable.

4.

Concerning the integrality question we discussed in
3
, let us conclude with loose ends.
We assume the existence of the resolution of singularities.
Let
k
be an algebraically closed field,
S=\mathrm{Spec}(k)
,
T
be a parameter space with generic point
\eta
,
and let
\mathrm{pr}\colon X\times_{S}T\rightarrow T
.
Let
\xi
be a closed point of
X\times_{S}\eta
such that the extension
k(\xi)/k(\eta)
is
inseparable
.
The sheaf
\Lambda_{\xi}
belongs to
\mathrm{Fl\acute{E}t}_{0}(X\times_{S}T/T)
,
but we need a denominator when we consider
d_{\mathrm{pr}}(\Lambda_{\xi})
using the notation of Lemma
4.9
.
However, if we replace
T
by its suitable relative Frobenius over
S
, we may make
\xi
separable, and remove the denominator.
Pursuing this idea, it seems possible to construct a localization system without denominators,
and show the integral version of the non-microlocalized pushforward formula.
Now, let
T^{(m)*}X:=T^{*}X\times_{X,\mathrm{Frob}^{m}}X
, where
\mathrm{Frob}^{m}
is the
m
-th Frobenius endomorphism of
X
.
Then we have the Frobenius morphisms
T^{*}X\rightarrow T^{(1)*}X\rightarrow T^{(2)*}X\rightarrow\dots
relative to
X
.
A natural place for the microlocalization seems to be
T^{(m)*}X
for large enough
m
.
On this level, it seems possible to show the integral version of the microlocalized pushforward formula.
However, for a closed subscheme
Z\subset T^{*}X
, the homomorphism
\mathrm{CH}_{\dim(X)}(Z)\rightarrow\mathrm{CH}_{\dim(X)}(i_{m}(Z))
,
where
i_{m}\colon T^{*}X\rightarrow T^{(m)*}X
, may have non-trivial kernel (which vanishes if we invert
p
)
unless we have some constraint on
Z
.

6
